# Clean room

Margo is written clean room. This page says what that means, how the 2026-10-24 rewrites were done, and where every
lighting and post-processing formula in the engine comes from.

## The rule

Scott's rule (2026-10-24): Margo never uses files from other repositories, whatever their licence. Code is written from public specifications, papers and
descriptions; nobody copies, adapts or reads another engine's or application's source while writing the matching
Margo code. Other engines run only where they live (`../engine-bench`). The one exception is large standard files
every engine uses (sample models, test assets, prebuilt decoders), each recorded in
[THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md](../THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md). The `clean-room` quality gate
([conventions.md](conventions.md#quality-gate)) fails a new mention of another graphics library in `packages/**`
or a new third-party dependency.

Rulings on what counts (Scott, 2026-10-24):

- **Published engine standards are kept, with a citation.** A formula or constant an engine team published for
  others to use, for example Karis 2014's mobile environment BRDF fit and the 0.045 minimum perceptual roughness of
  the Filament design document. Unreal and Unity interop conventions are kept on the same footing.
- **MagicaVoxel parity fits are kept**, recorded as fitted to observed MagicaVoxel output. They were measured from
  the application's renders, never from its code; its renders are not in the repository.
- **Rewritten:** code that followed another engine's source, and library defaults copied with no physical basis.

## Process

Each area went through four steps, with a different agent at each step so no writer saw the old code:

1. **Dirty spec.** An auditor who may read the old Margo code (never another engine's) writes a words-only spec:
   files and line ranges, the public interface callers rely on, the required behaviour, which constants are
   binding (public sources) and which are the writer's to choose, and the tests, proofs and visual baselines that
   touch it. Specs hold no code.
2. **Stub.** The auditor removes the old body (and any built `dist` that held it), leaves a `CLEAN-ROOM:` marker
   and keeps everything outside the range unchanged, so the package still builds.
3. **Clean writer.** A fresh agent reads only the spec, the public sources it names and the tests it lists, and
   writes the body. It chooses its own internal constants and documents each one in a comment.
4. **Tests.** The package tests, proofs (`npm run proofs -- --current`) and visual checks run against the new body;
   any proof whose feature-off output changes is advanced with a note in
   [whitepaper/claims.md](whitepaper/claims.md).

## Rewrites of 2026-10-24

From commits ac465a00..ccb4a164 and their follow-ups, every area the 2026-10-21 audit found close to other engines' or
libraries' code:

- geometry (primitives, curves, extrusion, earcut triangulation, edges, convex hull, subdivision, utilities)
- controls (orbit, trackball, fly, first-person, transform)
- helpers; CSS3D labels; SDF text; eye-dome lighting
- math edge cases; BVH shapecast
- contact shadows; glitch and afterimage
- hashes and noise
- sheen, thin film, transmission, matcap, depth packing
- light falloff, LTC edge integral, cotangent frame
- ACES fit
- LZF, meshopt, EXR PIZ, USD LZ4 and integer coding, JT LZMA
- SVG, outline-font and MTL loaders
- ORCA avoidance and the path funnel (navigation removed; `@margoengine/navmesh/build` replaces it,
  [renames-2026-10-24.md](renames-2026-10-24.md))
- fixtures from other projects replaced by Margo-generated ones; three and Babylon dropped as dev dependencies

The provenance sweep that produced the table below added five more:

- FXAA, rebuilt from Lottes' 2009 whitepaper (it had followed the FXAA 3.11 reference shader)
- the transmission blur level, now derived from the GGX facet tilt and Snell's law
- the 0-15 voxel light-level curve, now equal CIE L* steps
- the Phong `refractionRatio` default (0.98 to air-to-glass 1/1.5) and the `rgbShift` amount default (0.005 to 0.002)

The FBX importer's Phong exponent to roughness mapping was also corrected to match materials and the exporters
(row EX2).

## Where each formula comes from

Every row of the four provenance tables (groups pbr, env, post and shadow-voxel; ids as in those tables), grouped by
the package of its first site. The source is a public paper, specification or standard; "Margo-own" with the design
it follows; "published engine standard" with its document; or "fitted to observed MagicaVoxel output". Most sites
carry the same citation as a one-line comment in the code.

### physics

The own solver's kernel (`packages/physics/kernel`, Rust, no dependencies). Nothing here comes from the source of Rapier, parry, Box2D, Jolt, Bullet, PhysX or any paper's companion repository; none was opened.

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PK1 | Bit-equal floats: every operator rounds to nearest, ties to even; NaN payloads are the one freedom, so no NaN may reach the state; no fused or relaxed operation is used | physics/kernel/src/math.rs, lib.rs | **Read:** the WebAssembly specification, *Execution: Numerics*, section Floating-Point Operations. The rule that follows from it (only + - * / sqrt, compares and integers) is the plan's |
| PK2 | Semi-implicit Euler: `v += h (g + F / m)`, then `x += h v` with the new velocity, so the velocity a body reports is the one that moved it | physics/kernel/src/integrate.rs | **Read:** Catto, "Iterative Dynamics with Temporal Coherence", GDC 2005, eqs. 1, 2, 36, 37, in full; Catto, "Numerical Methods", GDC 2015, in full. The rule that a force reaches every substep and an impulse lands once is Margo's, from its ragdoll findings |
| PK3 | A step split into n substeps, each one velocity update and one pose update; smaller steps beat more iterations for the same work | physics/kernel/src/integrate.rs | **Read:** Macklin, Storey, Lu, Terdiman, Chentanez, Jeschke, Müller, "Small Steps in Physics Simulation", SCA 2019, in full |
| PK4 | Gyroscopic term `I w' + w x I w = 0` in the principal frame, one implicit-midpoint step by Newton's method. The midpoint keeps kinetic energy and the size of angular momentum because both are quadratic in w. A step may not raise the energy | physics/kernel/src/integrate.rs | **Read:** Catto, "Numerical Methods", GDC 2015, for working in the body frame and Newton's method on the implicit step; Hairer, Geometric Numerical Integration lecture notes, week 2, in full (https://www.unige.ch/~hairer/poly_geoint/week2.pdf), for implicit midpoint keeping quadratic invariants. The rest of those notes was not read. The scale-back to the starting energy is Margo's |
| PK5 | Damping as implicit Euler of `v' = -c v`: the factor `1 / (1 + c h)` stays in (0, 1] for every c and h | physics/kernel/src/integrate.rs | Own derivation, one line from the definition of implicit Euler |
| PK6 | Rotation by the exact exponential of `h w`: Maclaurin series of `sin(a/2)/a` and `cos(a/2)` in `a^2`, turns over a radian halved and squared back; its inverse from the series of `asin(s)/s` and the half-angle rule | physics/kernel/src/math.rs | Own derivation from the Maclaurin series, plus and times only, so bit-equal everywhere. The plan named a first-order quaternion update; it was replaced because its turn is not `h w`, so the reported velocity would not be the one that moved the body, which is what the ragdoll findings asked for |
| PK7 | Bodies and colliders in free lists, last freed first, each row with a generation: which row a call gets depends only on the calls before it | physics/kernel/src/world.rs | Margo's own, for replay; the plan names the free list |
| PK8 | Mass of a box `8abc rho`, a ball `4/3 pi r^3 rho`, a capsule as a cylinder plus two hemispheres. Moments: `m (b^2 + c^2) / 3` for a box, `2/5 m r^2` for a ball. For the capsule, the cylinder's `m r^2 / 2` along it and `m (r^2 / 4 + l^2 / 12)` across it; a hemisphere's centroid `3r/8` from its flat face, moved by the parallel axis theorem, gives `m (2/5 r^2 + a^2 + 3/4 a r)` across | physics/kernel/src/mass.rs | Own derivation from the integrals that define mass and moment of inertia, checked against a numeric integral of each solid in tests/bodies.rs |
| PK9 | Principal axes by cyclic Jacobi rotations, using `sqrt` and division only, each plane rotation joined to the frame as a quaternion by its half angle, so the frame stays a proper rotation and the result is bit-equal everywhere | physics/kernel/src/mass.rs | Own derivation from the definition of a Jacobi rotation, the textbook method for a symmetric matrix. No source was copied |
| PK10 | Sweep and prune: one sorted list of box ends per axis, kept from step to step, so sorting is a short walk; an overlap starts or stops only where a min end and a max end change places. Boxes are written first and each crossing asks the final boxes, so the result does not depend on the order ends walk in. Fat boxes extended by a small margin, so a pair is found before contact | physics/kernel/src/broad.rs | **Read:** Cohen, Lin, Manocha, Ponamgi, "I-COLLIDE: An Interactive and Exact Collision Detection System for Large-Scale Environments", I3D 1995, in full (sections 4.2 and 6.3 for this row). Baraff's 1992 thesis, the plan's other source for sort and sweep, was not read: Cornell eCommons holds it (TR 92-1275) as a scan of 220 page images with no text layer, and no tool here could read it. The final-box rule, the pair rows, and the batched join (a stable merge sort, one merge, one sweep with new and old boxes in two open lists) are Margo's |
| PK11 | A step's motion predicted from velocity and the whole step's time, and collision detection once per frame reused by every substep | physics/kernel/src/broad.rs | **Read:** Macklin et al. 2019, section 4.2. The bound `a dt^2` over substeps and the chord bound for a turn are own derivations |
| PK12 | Pair lookup by open addressing with linear probing and backward-shift deletion, pair rows held on two linked lists | physics/kernel/src/broad.rs | Textbook data structures, written from their definitions. No source was copied |
| PK13 | Islands are contact groups: connected components of the contact graph. Fixed and scripted bodies are insulators and join no group, so a floor does not tie two piles together | physics/kernel/src/island.rs | **Read:** Erleben, "Stable, Robust, and Versatile Multibody Dynamics Animation", PhD thesis, University of Copenhagen, 2005, chapter 5 and section 6.5, from the copy in the Internet Archive of image.diku.dk. The other chapters were not read. In P1 two bodies touch when their fat boxes overlap; the narrow phase's flag replaces that in P2 |
| PK14 | Union-find as a compressed forest: linking by size, halving on find | physics/kernel/src/island.rs | **Not read:** Tarjan, "Efficiency of a Good But Not Linear Set Union Algorithm", JACM 22(2), 1975: behind the ACM's paywall; its 1972 Cornell report (eCommons 1813/5942) is a scan with no text layer, and no tool here could read it. **Read instead:** Jayanti and Tarjan, "Concurrent Disjoint Set Union", arXiv 2003.01203, 2020, in full, which state the sequential structure and its linking and compaction rules |
| PK15 | Sleep policy: kinetic energy per unit of mass, which weighs linear and turning motion in one number, under a threshold held across a window, with no rise in energy across that window, so a ball at the top of its flight and a body gathering speed slowly stay awake; a group sleeps only when all its bodies are ready; a body whose contacts go counts its window again | physics/kernel/src/island.rs | **Read:** Erleben 2005, section 6.5. That a body under a force is not still follows its definition of a sleepy object. The window, the threshold values and the ring of sleepers are Margo's |
| PK16 | The replay's fingerprint: FNV-1a over a world's 32-bit words, offset basis 2166136261 and prime 16777619, each word taken whole where the published hash takes a byte. A round is a bijection of the hash, so one changed word always changes the result; a fingerprint for a test, never a defence against a chosen input | physics/kernel/src/world.rs | **Read:** Fowler, Noll, Vo, Eastlake, Hansen, "The FNV Non-Cryptographic Hash Algorithm", Internet-Draft draft-eastlake-fnv-35, sections 1 to 7. Its source code sections 8 and on were not opened |
| PK17 | The replay scene's stream: 32-bit xorshift with shifts 13, 17, 5, period 2^32 - 1. Integers only, so it is the same in Rust and JavaScript | physics/kernel/tests/replay.rs, physics/tests/replay-scene.js | **Read:** Marsaglia, "Xorshift RNGs", Journal of Statistical Software 8(14), 2003, in full |
| PK18 | One-shot manifolds: a separating axis test over face normals and edge crosses, with a bias towards faces (a flat triangle's edge arcs split in two, PK28); the incident face is the most anti-parallel one, clipped against the reference face's side planes, points kept below the face and moved onto it; an edge contact is one point midway between the closest points. Hull data as half-edges, a box as an 8-vertex hull, no coplanar neighbours | physics/kernel/src/sat.rs, narrow.rs, hull.rs | **Read:** Gregorius, "Robust Contact Creation for Physics Simulations", GDC 2015, Valve PDF, in full with its speaker notes. **Not read:** Gregorius, "The Separating Axis Test between Convex Polyhedra", GDC 2013: the GDC Vault copy answers 403, the archive 429, and Valve's page does not list it. That the edge test needs only pairs that make a face of the Minkowski difference is named by the 2015 talk; the arc crossing test is written from that definition. The clipper is Sutherland and Hodgman's, named by the same talk, from its definition |
| PK19 | Hull mass: tetrahedra from an interior point to each face's triangle fan, `integral x_i = V/4 sum p_i`, `integral x_i x_j = V/20 (sum p_i p_j + sum p_i sum p_j)`, then the parallel axis theorem back to the centroid. The plan names Mirtich 1996 for polyhedra; that paper was not used | physics/kernel/src/hull.rs | Own derivation from the integrals over a tetrahedron, checked against a cube and a corner tetrahedron in tests/sat.rs |
| PK20 | Distance between convex shapes by the GJK descent with the Signed Volumes sub-algorithm in place of Johnson's, through one support function; depth by an expanding polytope grown from the final simplex, a triangle by its taller side alone, so the solid stays convex when a core lies on a face; a capsule's two points clipped against a face's side planes or the other core's ends | physics/kernel/src/gjk.rs, narrow.rs | **Read:** Montanari, Petrinic, Barbieri, "Improving the GJK Algorithm for Faster and More Reliable Distance Queries Between Convex Objects", ACM TOG 36(3), 2017, in full. Where the code differs from it: every face of a simplex is inspected, where the paper skips those without the newest vertex (the same answer, a speed matter for the quiet pass); a segment past its ends takes the nearer end, not always the newest; areas take differences first, not its sum of products, which keeps more bits for a small triangle far from the origin; a descent that stops shrinking by rounding keeps its last simplex, a guard the paper does not need at double precision. Its exact predicates (section 4.3) are left out, as it found them a modest gain. Gregorius, GDC 2015 (above), slides 76 to 78 for capsules. **Not read:** van den Bergen, "Proximity Queries and Penetration Depth Computation on 3D Game Objects", GDC 2001, which the plan names for EPA: unreachable, 403 and 429 from every copy tried, so the expanding polytope is written from the method's name and the support-function view of Montanari |
| PK21 | Warm starting across steps: a new point takes the impulses of the old point with its id, else of the nearest old point on body A within 2 cm, each old point used once. The impulse carries as one world vector and is split along the new normal, and scales with the substep's length, as an impulse is a force times a substep. A deterministic tangent with no trigonometry, from the normal's two larger components | physics/kernel/src/contact.rs | **Read:** Catto, "Iterative Dynamics with Temporal Coherence", GDC 2005, and his 2006 slides, in full, for warm starting and feature ids; Gregorius 2015 for coherent features. Own derivation for the world-vector split and the substep ratio |
| PK22 | Four points of many: the deepest, the farthest from it, the largest triangle on that edge, then the point adding the most area outside the triangle, with signed areas and no square roots. The third point is taken by size with the edge turned to suit, where the talk takes it by sign. Persistence: last step's ids and feature win ties, 5 percent on area and distance, 1 mm on depth and on the face test | physics/kernel/src/narrow.rs, sat.rs | **Read:** Gregorius, GDC 2015, in full with its notes, for the reduction and for coherent features. Own choice of the hysteresis numbers, measured in tests/reduce.rs |
| PK23 | The contact solver: soft step (substeps with a soft bias pass, a relax pass, warm starting), the soft constraint in implicit form, speculative points, friction before the normal in a circular cone. Anchored friction as a world-vector drift pulled back in the soft pass and tallied once the poses have moved, from the speeds they moved with, kept at the cone's edge when the cone saturates. As many relax passes as soft ones; the relax pass solves the normal first. A manifold's normal rows swept to convergence within a pass (block Gauss-Seidel: Gauss-Seidel over pairs, each pair's rows solved to its own fixed point), each sweep the one-point update on the points' speeds through the manifold's J M^-1 J^T, the soft rows being Gauss-Seidel on K plus the diagonal K_ii / a2, own derivation from the soft constraint's form. A manifold of four points ends each sweep with the exact minimum of the rows' quadratic along the one impulse pattern that moves no body (sum zero, no turn about either body: the four-dimensional cross product of the rows of ones and of the points' turn arms along two tangents), cut to the bounds, as Gauss-Seidel settles that pattern only through the leak; own derivation, from J having rank 3 for points of one plane. Restitution after relaxing from the approach speed of a point struck in that substep, above two substeps of gravity; a point struck across an open gap takes the speed it meets the surface with, v^2 = v0^2 + 2 a d under gravity alone (constant-acceleration kinematics). Inside the slop a biased pass treats the point as speculative toward the slop's depth, bias (gap + slop) / h on a rigid row, the soft row only past it, as Catto's Solver2D post describes the soft step's separation plus linear slop. A ball's or capsule's gap read from its core segment's nearest point plus its radius along the normal, as a round shape's surface point toward a plane is (own, from the geometry), the lever arm left at the step's contact point. Own, from measurement: the manifold's first point turns each substep, as from a fixed first point a 1:1000 stack walks 3.8 mm; contact stiffness capped at a quarter of the substep rate; at one pass a substep a pair of two dynamic bodies, neither dominating, capped at an eighth while it carries under 50 times the lighter body's weight, as one pass left a 20-row pile rocking at a quarter at every damping ratio from 1 to 40 (the eighth from Catto's Solver2D post, which keeps the stiffness well below the step rate, 30 Hz for a 60 Hz step; a stiffness per kind of pair as Rapier's documentation describes for static contacts, its documented behaviour only, read on docs.rs; the load bound in the spirit of setting a contact's stiffness from the overlap it may take under load, Cundall and Strack 1979; Box2D v3 and Rapier run only as black boxes on our own scenes, outputs read); 8 substeps and 2 passes | physics/kernel/src/solver.rs | **Read:** Catto, Solver2D post, 2024, in full (the end of its performance text was cut off); Catto, "Soft Constraints", GDC 2011, skimmed by lines; Muller et al., "Detailed Rigid Body Simulation with Extended Position Based Dynamics", 2020, in full; Catto, "Continuous Collision", GDC 2013, in full; Catto, "Understanding Constraints", GDC 2014, in full. Where the code differs from Muller: it works on velocities with soft constraints, not on positions, so it freezes each normal for a step where the paper finds it again for every projection; it takes one friction coefficient, not static and dynamic; its static friction adds up the slip as a drift, where the paper cancels one substep's slip and lets go past the cone; the bounce threshold, two substeps of gravity, and the restitution of a point from its approach speed are the paper's equation 34. The speculative point's two costs named by Catto 2013 are met as follows: the restitution needing special handling is the relax rule in `solve_contacts`; ghost collisions off a point the body never reaches are open (`docs/limits.md`). No other solver's source |
| PK24 | Joints: one six-axis row with each axis locked, limited, driven or free. Locks along a path of joints by block Cholesky of a block tridiagonal J M^-1 J^T, in doubles, the sparsity Baraff 1996 uses; bias of the whole error, then a velocity projection on rows from the new pose, the two stages of RATTLE. Motors and springs as soft rows in implicit form, stiffness and damping per unit of effective mass in the acceleration model. Hinge alignment by a1 x a2, angle read along the axis, unwrapped by a tally; shortest arc to a joint's axis. Own, from derivation and measurement: the pushing pass, the one rule that keeps a rigid row with a warm start stable. Take one mode of J M^-1 J^T that a pass shrinks by g, with share s, error e, y = h J v and p = h a lambda the memory. Remembering every impulse gives p' = y' - y, so as g nears 1 the substep's matrix nears a Jordan block of three, e from y from p, and the only feedback is the error, of size (1 - g) s. Then mu^3 = -(1 - g) s, two of whose roots have a positive real part: the mode grows at any share, as a triple integrator does under position feedback alone. Measured on the 8 by 8 grid: speed triples each 10 steps from step 30, gap 1.5 m, 100 m/s; with 8 passes in place of 2 the same matrix gives 1e-5 m, so the singular matrix is not the cause; without warm starting 8e-4 m. Keeping the bias out of the memory removes the feedback path from the error to the memory, and the model's spectral radius falls under 1 for every g and s tried; measured 1.8e-4 m at 8 by 8 and 9.5e-4 m at 30 by 30. Soft rows have a way out of their own, as gamma lifts the slowest mode. Limits are rigid up to their edge and soft past it, the contact's Soft Step row (Catto 2011 form, per unit of effective mass), measured: with a rigid push a limb on a floor past its cone moved at 0.40 m/s for good, with the soft one it sleeps. A limit's row is the Schur complement of its joint's own locks, t M^-1 t^T - c^T K^-1 c with c = J M^-1 t^T, and it reads the speed those locks leave, own derivation; without it a rod came off its stop at a quarter of its speed, since the locks take back most of each impulse on the free body and the warm start remembers what the passes did not settle. A rope and a spring: a joint's first axis made the line between the anchors, reading their distance, with a limit row or a motor row in the force model; Muller et al. 2020 Eq 25 for the maximum distance, Catto 2011 for the spring. A spherical motor: three motor rows on the rotation vector left to the target, the target being frame A moved onto the rotation asked for; Tan, Liu and Turk 2011 ask the same of a PD servo, the control force from the next state, (M + Kd dt)^-1 in their Eq 11, of which this row's gamma is the scalar form, and their stability condition Kd >= Kp dt does not bind here, as the row is implicit in position too. Their M is the joint-space inertia, which a limit's Schur complement stands for here; a motor's free-body row lands within 0.0006 rad of the closed form as measured, because the passes converge on the row's fixed point, so motors stayed as they were. A cone: swing and twist split of the frame turn, q = swing twist (Dobrowolski 2015), twist 2 atan2(q.x, q.w) with its rate (a1 + a2) . w / (2 (x^2 + w^2)) from differentiating that angle, swing as an ellipse of the rotation vector's Y and Z parts, the row along the gradient measuring the first-order distance past the edge, own derivation. The share of 0.2 for paths that meet by Gauss-Seidel is a measured choice with room: 0.05 to 0.7 all hold a 30 by 30 grid. Rows that depend on each other: K = J M^-1 J^T is not formed; with F F^T = M^-1 per body (the roots of the inverse masses, the Cholesky factor of the inverse inertia), K = A^T A for A = F^T J^T, and Householder QR of A with column pivoting gives R, K's Cholesky factor with complete pivoting (Higham 1990, section 5.2, his (5.2.2)), solved as R^T y = b, R x = y (Higham 2008). Along a path A is block banded, so it is factored front by front: a joint's front is what the joint before left free on the body they share, compressed by a QR of those rows' parts on that body to at most six, and the six rows of its other body; its pivot is the column with the most of its own squared length left, complete pivoting on the matrix scaled to a unit diagonal, as linear and turning rows share no unit; the stop is Higham 1990's test (4.3) at his suggested n u, n the rows of the path, u f32's epsilon, as the rows are f32. The reflection's sign is the one that does not cancel. Why not K: eliminating it joint by joint puts W^T E W into each Schur complement (his Lemma 2.1, W = K11^-1 K12), and with no pivoting across joints W has any size; measured on joint web seed 1, a 13-row path on 12 freedoms with a 43 m spherical joint, the last pivot read 4e-7 of its size where the f32 rows give exactly 0 in rationals (so not the f32 inputs), 4.8e6 rad/s; seed 18 before it, 10 rows on 6 freedoms, Schur noise 1e-7, 3.8e8 m/s. The front by front order and the compression are our own arrangement of banded QR; the stop at n u in place of u alone is Higham's suggestion, and it also drops rows within about sqrt(n u) radians of depending. A push near such a pose: Buss and Kim 2005's bound gamma = min(1, N / M) gamma_max, from their section 4, with N what a path asks, M the fastest any body would go at its centre plus its arm, on the rise in a body's turn and in its centre over its arm. Where the code differs: they work per singular vector and bound the change itself, this cuts the whole path, as a path solve has no decomposition, and takes gamma_max 0.2 for their pi / 4, measured (taut chain 39 m/s at pi / 4; the chain of fifty first cut at 0.1). A first version bounded speed alone, at N, and it acted on 99 percent of a plain pendulum's substeps, cutting pushes to 0.37: the counters showed it, `a_small_gap_closes_in_one_push` holds that. A cut on the push alone left a limit's kick standing with the locks' answer to it cut, 1,000 times the energy, seen as a NaN at step 2,690 of one seed, so the cut reads the rise and not the gain. The bias cap is their ClampMag, its turning term their D_max,i = d_i + D_max, and r w h covers the r (h w)^2 / 2 a turn opens up to a turn of 2 radians. The warm start's line search is our own, the exact minimum of f(a) = a m^T J v + a^2 m^T K m / 2, with m^T K m read off as what the memory did to its own rows; a load that is right has its minimum at 1. An always-on search broke a light link and a chain on limits, so it runs only past one push's energy, a threshold set by measurement. | physics/kernel/src/joints.rs | **Read:** Baraff, "Linear-Time Dynamics using Lagrange Multipliers", SIGGRAPH 1996, in full, as extracted text; Hairer, Geometric Numerical Integration notes, section V.5 only, for RATTLE (Andersen 1983, not read itself); Catto, "Soft Constraints", GDC 2011, in full; Muller et al. 2020, in full, their Eq 20, Eq 21 and Algorithm 3; Dobrowolski, "Swing-twist decomposition in Clifford algebra", 2015, in full, Proposition 2 for the shortest arc and the split a cone uses; Wang, Servin and Berglund, "Warm starting the projected Gauss-Seidel algorithm for granular matter simulation", 2015, in full. Where the code differs: Baraff factors bodies and constraints together for trees and treats loops as auxiliary rows, while this code factors only serial paths and leaves branches and loops to Gauss-Seidel; Wang et al. take 85 percent of the last multiplier, found best by trial with no stability analysis, and a fraction was measured here too (0.9 holds 8 by 8 but left a 30 by 30 grid moving at 1.6 m/s), so the memory stays whole and the bias stays out of it. Tan, Liu and Turk, "Stable Proportional-Derivative Controllers", IEEE CG&A 2011, in full, as extracted text; Higham, "Analysis of the Cholesky Decomposition of a Semi-Definite Matrix", 1990 (MIMS EPrint 2008.56), in full, as extracted text, read again 2026-11-08 for Lemma 2.1, section 4 and section 5.2; Higham, "Cholesky Factorization", 2009 (MIMS EPrint 2008.116), in full; the Wikipedia article "Householder transformation", its QR section, for the reflection and its sign; Buss, "Introduction to Inverse Kinematics with Jacobian Transpose, Pseudoinverse and Damped Least Squares methods", 2009, in full; Buss and Kim, "Selectively Damped Least Squares for Inverse Kinematics", 2005, in full, the paper and not its source code. Andrews, Teichmann and Kry, "Geometric Stiffness for Real-time Constrained Multibody Dynamics", Computer Graphics Forum 36(2) 2017, in full as extracted text, for the cause of the level chain's stretch in `docs/known-bugs.md` (a pull's turning read explicitly, their section 1.2 and the stability bound h^2 k / m < 4 of their section 4.1); nothing of it is built, and its adaptive damping is claimed by US patent 10,713,400 B2. **Not read:** Golub and Van Loan, Matrix Computations, and Higham, Accuracy and Stability of Numerical Algorithms (books not to hand; nothing here rests on them beyond what the two Higham papers state); Householder 1958 and Businger and Golub 1965, known by citation only; Tournier, Nesme, Gilles and Faure, "Stable Constrained Dynamics", 2015, known through Andrews et al. only; Tomcin, Sibbing and Kobbelt 2014, whose PDF could not be fetched; Cline and Pai 2003 on post-stabilization, likewise; no text on trust regions as such. Nothing here rests on them. No engine source |
| PK25 | Events and filters: a pair's touching is compared with what it found last step and each change listed, two words an event; groups are the root entry's own `collisionGroups` packing, 16 bits of memberships and 16 of filter, a pair meeting when each one's memberships cross the other's filter; a sensor pair only finds whether it overlaps; dominance is a bit on the pair read where its two bodies are fetched, between dynamic bodies only; a disabled body or collider leaves every pair and returns with its state | physics/kernel/src/events.rs, narrow.rs | **Read:** none; bookkeeping with no paper behind it, written from the root entry's own documented behaviour in `packages/physics/README.md` |
| PK26 | Convex hulls from points: Quickhull, each point in the outside set of a face it lies above, the farthest point of a set added next, the faces it sees replaced by a fan to their horizon (the paper's simplified Beneath-Beyond, Theorem 2.1), the first tetrahedron from axis extremes. Fat planes and the horizon as a depth first walk that yields one counter-clockwise loop, from Gregorius. Own: a face counts as seen when the eye spans a positive volume with its three corners, past Shewchuk's stage A bound on that volume's rounding, (7e + 56e^2) times the permanent of the same expansion with e = 2^-53 (until 2026-11-07 an own, wider bound of 45 ulp of the product of the three L1 lengths, which called a point a hair above a wide face not above it), so neighbouring faces agree on what the eye sees; until 2026-11-07 a face counted as seen unless the eye was clearly below its rounded plane, which folded the fan over a face it kept on Thingi10K meshes 40261 and 41940 and left hulls that were not convex. An eye on the line of a horizon edge takes the face beyond it too. Two faces merge only where their outlines meet along one run of edges, so no merged face is pinched at a corner; triangles merge after the build, grown from the largest over neighbours within a quarter of the kernel's FLAT of its supporting plane, which leaves no holes as a convex surface under a supporting plane is bounded from below by its edge, a triangle touching only at a corner waiting for a second shared edge; then neighbours the kernel calls one plane merge in independent sets sorted by angle, the paper's order. The kernel now calls two faces one plane only when every corner lies within half of FLAT of the other's plane, as an angle alone refused real creases of random clouds | physics/src/next/hull.ts; physics/kernel/src/hull.rs | **Read:** Barber, Dobkin and Huhdanpaa, "The Quickhull Algorithm for Convex Hulls", ACM TOMS 22(4) 1996, in full as extracted text, except its Figure 1 outline, which is a picture the extraction lost; Gregorius, "Implementing Quickhull", GDC 2014, the speaker notes in full as extracted text, not the slide pictures, so none of its code snippets. Where the code differs: the paper and Gregorius merge during the build, this code builds triangles with fat planes and merges after, and keeps every corner at a given point rather than moving planes. Shewchuk, "Adaptive Precision Floating-Point Arithmetic and Fast Robust Geometric Predicates", Discrete and Computational Geometry 18(3) 1997, sections 1 and 4.4 of the short paper, and Tables 1 and 3 of the long technical report (CMU-CS-96-140, robust-arithmetic.ps from the Quake project papers), as extracted text, for filtering a determinant's sign by a bound on its rounding; the ORIENT3D stage A bound of Table 3 is used as given, and no exact arithmetic is used, so a volume within the bound counts as none. Not read: the qhull program and the Java implementation Gregorius names; no engine source |
| PK27 | Body kinds, a body's own solid and extra passes: the own solid added to the colliders by the parallel axis theorem, m (\|d\|^2 E - d d^T), and its principal moments rotated in as R D R^T, both already in `mass.rs`; a body made fixed leaves its fixed pairs and one made moving finds them by one walk over the sorted ends; an island asking for e extra passes takes the last iterations + e of iterations + the most asked, so the pushing pass stays the last for all and the island runs as that world would, bit for bit. The generic joint is the six-axis row PK24 already solves, its locks, limits and motors named by axis | physics/kernel/src/mass.rs, island.rs, integrate.rs; physics/src/next/body.ts, joint.ts | **Read:** none new; the parallel axis theorem is textbook mechanics, the rest is bookkeeping written from the root entry's documented behaviour in `packages/physics/README.md` and from Rapier's outputs through its npm API (mass, centre and inertia after each call), never its source |
| PK28 | Triangle meshes: a tree over the triangles built top down by the binned surface area heuristic, 16 bins on the widest axis of the centroids' box, the cheapest of the 15 cuts by A_L N_L + A_R N_R, no cut that costs no less than the leaf, children's boxes the unions of their bins so a level reads each triangle once, a node past depth 48 or with no centroid width halved by count. One manifold for each touching triangle as a part of the mesh pair. Ghost collisions: an edge is convex, concave, flat or open from the triangle across it; a normal leaning over a flat or concave edge is snapped to the face, one past a convex edge's neighbour is skipped, one in the arc between the faces stands, read from the side the normal comes from. The separating axis test splits a flat shape's edge arc, half a great circle, at the edge's outward side so the arc crossing test serves triangles | physics/kernel/src/bvh.rs, mesh.rs, sat.rs; physics/src/kernel.ts | **Read:** Wald, "On fast Construction of SAH-based Bounding Volume Hierarchies", IEEE Symposium on Interactive Ray Tracing 2007, in full; Catto, "Ghost Collisions", box2d.org blog, in full, written for chains of edges in 2D and carried here to a triangle's three edges; Gregorius 2015 (PK18) for the arcs. Own: the fold tolerance, the lean tolerance, the two-sided reading, leaves of two, the median fallback. No engine source |
| PK29 | Height fields: a mesh row with vertex rows only, Rapier's layout (rows along z, columns along x, heights column major, centred), each cell two triangles split from (r + 1, c) to (r, c + 1); a triangle's corners, the triangle across each edge and the edge classes follow from its cell, and the cells under a box are found by halving the column and row lines, then culled by their corner heights. The triangles meet shapes through PK28's code, so a part's manifold is the same bits as on the same triangles held as rows | physics/kernel/src/mesh.rs; physics/src/kernel.ts | Own, from PK28's papers. The layout and the diagonal were taken from Rapier's public `heightfield` documentation and checked by casting rays at Rapier 0.21's field through the root entry, outputs only. No engine source |
| PK30 | Compound colliders: each convex part is a collider row of its own off the broad phase and off its body's chain, posed in the body's frame through the compound's pose, with its own fat box written by the broad phase; a compound pair keeps a part row for each touching child, child pair or child and triangle, named by two words, so the pair's events, touching and force speak for one collider; the body's mass adds the children's solids at the compound's density | physics/kernel/src/compound.rs; physics/kernel/src/world.rs; physics/kernel/src/broad.rs; physics/kernel/src/mass.rs; physics/src/next/shapes.ts | Own composition of PK24 to PK28's routines, no new method and no paper of its own. Mass, centre, inertia and rest were checked against Rapier 0.21's `compound` through the root entry, outputs only. No engine source |
| PK31 | Voxel grids: a grid is a mesh row flagged MESH_VOXELS with a byte a cell (filled, and which of its six face neighbours are filled, across a coupled grid's border too); each filled cell with an exposed face is a box met through the convex routines, a part per touching cell; a contact normal that leans past 1e-4 toward a filled neighbour is snapped to the cell's nearest exposed face, and a cell with no exposed face gives nothing; `combineVoxelStates` and `propagateVoxelChange` write the neighbour bits across a border; `setVoxel` grows the grid; a dynamic grid weighs its cells | physics/kernel/src/voxel.rs; physics/kernel/src/mesh.rs; physics/kernel/src/mass.rs; physics/src/next/shapes.ts; physics/src/next/collider.ts | Reuse of the published ghost-collision rule (Catto 2020, "Ghost Collisions", box2d.org, read in full for PK28): the grid's exposed faces are the surface and the grid's face adjacency gives each seam's class (manager's decision 2026-11-07). That only surface voxels, not interior ones, give contacts is the voxmap of McNeely, Puterbaugh and Troy 1999 ("Six degree-of-freedom haptic rendering using voxel sampling", SIGGRAPH), known here from public descriptions of it, not read in full; nothing else was taken from it. Rapier 0.21's `voxels` was run through the root entry, outputs only: what a grid meets, growth on `setVoxel`, mass after an edit, the catch at an uncoupled border. No engine source |

### physics2d

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PH1 | Marching squares: 16 cases by a 4-bit index, a point on each crossed grid edge by linear interpolation, either choice in the two saddle cases gives closed contours | physics2d/src/outline.ts | **Read:** the Contouring part of chapter 6 of *The VTK Book* (W. Schroeder, K. Martin, B. Lorensen; the book's online text, not the toolkit's source), through a summary with short quotes; the Wikipedia article "Marching squares" for the saddle rule by the mean of the four corners. **Not read:** C. Maple, "Geometric Design and Space Planning Using the Marching Squares and Marching Cube Algorithms", GMAG 2003, DOI 10.1109/GMAG.2003.1219671, behind the publisher's paywall; only its abstract and citation were seen. The linking of segments into loops by grid edge is our own |
| PH2 | Line thinning: split a chain at its farthest vertex while that distance is over the tolerance | physics2d/src/outline.ts | **Read:** J. Hershberger and J. Snoeyink, "Speeding Up the Douglas-Peucker Line-Simplification Algorithm", UBC TR-92-07, 1992, sections 1 and 2 with Algorithm 1 (the rest of the paper is its faster method, not used here), which gives the method in full and states it equals the paper's Method 2. **Not read:** D. Douglas and T. Peucker, *The Canadian Cartographer* 10(2), 1973, 112-122, DOI 10.3138/FM57-6770-U75U-7727: the publisher refused and no open copy was found; a Hull repository copy of Whyatt and Wade's 1988 tutorial sits behind a bot check and was left alone. Cutting a closed loop at two far-apart points is our own |
| PH3 | Convex pieces: ear clipping, then a greedy merge across each diagonal that leaves both ends convex | physics2d/src/outline.ts | Ear clipping as in geometry's triangulate. The merge is credited to S. Hertel and K. Mehlhorn, "Fast triangulation of simple polygons", FCT 1983, LNCS 158, and its bound of four times the fewest pieces, by secondary descriptions only (the CGAL manual's prose, seen through a search summary). **Not read:** that paper; the 1985 journal version at pure.mpg.de sits behind a bot check and the publisher's copy refused; the scanned chapter of Mehlhorn's *Data Structures and Algorithms 3* on his page has no extractable text and no page renderer was on this machine. The merge here is written from the rule as stated, makes no claim of that bound, and is greedy where the published order may differ |

| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TM1 | Cell layouts: isometric boxes at x = (c - r + rows - 1) w / 2, y = (c + r) h / 2; staggered and hexagonal cells step (size + side) / 2 along the stagger axis with every other row or column pushed half a cell; outlines, picking by outline containment, edge neighbours, back-to-front paint order; hex cube coordinates, distance as the largest cube difference, cube rounding, rings and lines by interpolated cube points | tilemap/src/layout.ts, tilemap/src/iso.ts, tilemap/src/hex.ts | The Tiled TMX map format reference (map `orientation`, `staggeraxis`, `staggerindex`, `hexsidelength`; tiles anchored bottom left) and Tiled's Global Tile IDs page (flip bits, the hexagonal 60 and 120 degree turns), read in full; A. Patel, "Hexagonal Grids", Red Blob Games, 2013 (offset and cube coordinates, distances, cube rounding, rings), read as far as the page text loads (its conversion and line code is generated and was not in the text read; ours is derived from its offset definitions and checked by tests). No other engine's or editor's map code was read |
| TM2 | Grid pathfinding: A* with priority = cost so far + an estimate that never exceeds the rest (octile or hex distance times the lowest cell cost), early exit when the goal is popped; Dijkstra's search from the goals for a distance field and next-cell links; corner steps only past open cells | tilemap/src/path.ts | A. Patel, "Introduction to the A* Algorithm", Red Blob Games, 2014 (frontier, came_from, cost_so_far, early exit on pop, admissible heuristics, distance and flow fields), read in full; it credits P. Hart, N. Nilsson and B. Raphael, IEEE Transactions on Systems Science and Cybernetics 4(2), 1968, whose only copy found was a scan that could not be read. No other engine's or library's path code was read |
| TM3 | Field of view: eight octants scanned row by row outward from the viewer's cell centre; each cell's slope range from its square's corners; a set of lit slope intervals, with a cell seen when it overlaps one by more than a point and a blocking cell cutting its open range out of the set; fog of war as visible and explored masks | tilemap/src/fov.ts | B. Bergstrom, "FOV using recursive shadowcasting", RogueBasin, 2001 (octants, outward row scans, start and end slopes that blockers narrow), read as a prose summary only; the interval set in place of the recursion, the corner-gap rule and the exact ray oracle in the tests are Margo's own. This implementation and its tests were written by a helper that never saw reference code for field of view: an earlier draft by another helper, who had seen the CC0 code on A. Ford's "Symmetric Shadowcasting" page, was deleted unread and nothing from that page is used. No other engine's or library's field of view code was read |
| PX1 | Pixel-art scaling: each output pixel covers the source interval from x n / w to (x + 1) n / w on each axis, so the texel holding its right end gets the share (right - floor(right)) w / n and the one before it the rest, mixed as a box filter of the nearest-upscaled picture; the Scale2x rule (a corner takes its two neighbours toward it when they match and the other two differ); the fitted rectangle in whole pixels | pixel/src/scale.ts, pixel/src/blit.ts | Margo's own derivation of box-filter coverage from the definitions; the corner rule from the Wikipedia article "Pixel-art scaling algorithms" (EPX, E. Johnston, LucasArts, about 1992: a corner takes the colour of its two flanking neighbours when they match, unless three or more of the four neighbours are the same; Scale2x and AdvMAME2x, about 2001, the same rule), read as a prose summary with its pseudocode skipped. No other engine's or emulator's shader was read |
| PX2 | CRT look: glass bent by c (1 + k c'^2) with each axis scaled by the square of the other; a beam of 1 - s (1 - sin^2(pi f)) across each art row (f the place inside the row); a three-column grille passing one colour each; glow as a 3 x 3 binomial blur (weights 1 2 1 from Pascal's triangle) added on top | pixel/src/crt.ts | Margo-own: a design for the look, from the general description of tube screens (curved glass, visible lines between rows, aperture grille); no paper and no other shader. No other engine's or emulator's CRT shader was read |
| PX3 | Palette cycling: entry from + i shows the colour that started at from + (i - rate t) wrapped in the run, mixed in Oklab between the two entries a fractional step lies between; palettes mixed entry by entry in Oklab | pixel/src/palette.ts | Margo-own: the colour cycling idea as generally described for 1980s paint programs and games (rotating a run of palette entries); the Oklab conversion already cited for the palette pass (B. Ottosson, "A perceptual color space for image processing", 2020). No other engine's or tool's cycling code was read |

### lightmap

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | exact unpolarised dielectric Fresnel | lightmap/src/optics.ts | Fresnel equations; PBRT 4e sec. 9.3 |
| L2 | Beer-Lambert transmittance | lightmap/src/optics.ts | Beer-Lambert |
| L3 | direct form factor cos_i cos_j A / (pi r^2) and radiometry modes | lightmap/src/global.ts | Goral et al. 1984; Cohen & Wallace 1993 |
| L4 | bounce gather by cosine-weighted hemisphere rays | lightmap/src/global.ts | Malley's method, PBRT 4e sec. A.5 |
| L5 | caustic photons: point power 4pi I, area power pi L A, Snell refraction, Fresnel split | lightmap/src/refracted.ts | photometry; PBRT 4e sec. 9.3 (cited line 3) |
| L6 | sRGB encode, defaults (texelsPerUnit 1, aoDistance 4), normal 0.5-bias encode, ray bias 1e-3 | lightmap/src/bake.ts | IEC 61966-2-1 |

The batch 168 irradiance measurement drives Cycles inside Blender 5.3.0 Beta as a black box (`C:\blender\blender.exe`,
`MARGO_BLENDER` overrides the path). The bake is the diffuse direct and indirect passes with the surface color left
out, as the Blender manual describes those passes (https://docs.blender.org/manual/en/latest/render/cycles/baking.html
and https://docs.blender.org/manual/en/latest/render/layers/passes.html). The renderer source was not opened. The box
is the published Cornell data (https://bowers.cornell.edu/computer-graphics/data): corners, the white, green and red
reflectance tables, and the emission knots at 400, 500, 600 and 700 nm. RGB is a band average of that table, not a
CIE match. Faces that are not parallelograms are snapped with `p2 = p1 + p3 - p0` on both sides.

### oit

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| O-01 | Weighted blended OIT weight | oit/src/index.ts | McGuire & Bavoil, "Weighted Blended Order-Independent Transparency", JCGT 2(2), 2013, eqs. 7-9 form (cited). Power, scale and the 1000 cap are Margo's, with the reason given in a comment |
| O-02 | Composite accum/revealage | oit/src/index.ts | McGuire & Bavoil 2013 |
| O-03 | MomentOIT: three trigonometric moments of the log-warped depth, mapped to the circle by exp((2 pi - theta) i (z + 1) / 2) with wrapping zone theta = pi/10; transmittance by Algorithm 2 (Toeplitz system by LDL^H, roots of the kernel polynomial, weights from the Vandermonde system), overestimation 0.25, continuous weights in the wrapping zone with the piecewise-linear psi (Equation 6), bias towards (1, 0, 0, 0) | oit/src/moments.ts | **Read:** Muenstermann, Krumpen, Klein and Peters, "Moment-Based Order-Independent Transparency", PACMCGIT 1(1), 2018 (I3D), Sections 3 to 5 and Algorithm 2, and its supplementary document, Section 1 and Table 1. The HLSL code the paper links was not opened. The cubic is solved by Cardano's formula with the larger cube candidate, one Newton step and a projection onto the circle (our own, after the paper's remark on avoiding cancellation); the bias 3e-3 for half floats is ours, measured, where the paper gives 6.5e-4 for 16-bit fixed point |

The relight by difference and the read-back shadow share are Margo-own |
| P-38 | Decal culling: frustum planes as sums and differences of the view-projection rows, bounding sphere against each plane, and a conservative screen-span bound r (\|row0.xyz\| + \|x\| \|row3.xyz\|) / (w - r \|row3.xyz\|) | post/src/decals.ts | Plane extraction after Gribb and Hartmann, "Fast Extraction of Viewing Frustum Planes from the World-View-Projection Matrix", 2001 (written from the method as commonly described; the paper was not re-read for this job); the span bound is Margo-own, from the quotient of the projected coordinates, and is checked against sampled spheres in packages/post/test/decals.test.js |
| P-39 | Stochastic rough SSR: GGX visible-normal sampling, orthonormal basis around the normal, per-pixel per-frame random numbers, reprojected history clamped to the neighbourhood mean plus or minus one standard deviation | post/src/ssr.ts | E. Heitz, "Sampling the GGX Distribution of Visible Normals", JCGT 7(4), 2018; T. Duff et al., "Building an Orthonormal Basis, Revisited", JCGT 6(1), 2017; M. Jarzynski and M. Olano, "Hash Functions for GPU Rendering" (pcg3d), JCGT 9(3), 2020; variance clipping after M. Salvi, "An Excursion in Temporal Supersampling", GDC 2016 (written from the method as published; the papers were not re-read for this job). The blend floor and the meeting of the stochastic and mirror paths are Margo-own |
| P-40 | Multi-bounce screen GI: the light at each ray hit read from the previous frame's GI result through the previous camera, one more bounce per frame | post/src/ssgi.ts | Margo-own: the series L = E + T L of the rendering equation (J. Kajiya, "The Rendering Equation", SIGGRAPH 1986) evaluated one term per frame; reprojection is linear algebra |
| P-41 | Anamorphic streaks: bright pass with a soft knee, horizontal chain of sparse 9-tap passes at strides 3^k, additive composite | post/src/anamorphic.ts | Margo-own: the dilated (a trous) filter chain after Holschneider, Kronland-Martinet, Morlet and Tchamitchian, "A real-time algorithm for signal analysis with the help of the wavelet transform", 1989 (method, from memory of the publication, not re-read); weights, sizes and the knee are Margo-own |
| P-42 | Gaussian and box blur: separable horizontal then vertical pass, Gaussian weights exp(-x^2 / (2 sigma^2)) with sigma = radius / 3 normalised over the taps, box weights 1 / (2 radius + 1), edge taps clamped, a region test per pixel | post/src/blur.ts | Margo-own: separability of the 2D Gaussian and box filters is standard signal processing (the 2D kernel is the product of two 1D kernels); sigma = radius / 3 and the region are Margo-own choices; no source was read for this job |
| P-43 | Lens model: radial distortion r(1 + k r^2) scaled so barrel keeps the corners, dirt lit by an eighth-size thresholded glow blurred by a separable Gaussian, a built-in smudge pattern from an integer hash | post/src/lens.ts | Radial distortion after D. C. Brown, "Decentering Distortion of Lenses", Photogrammetric Engineering 32(3), 1966 (the one-term radial model, from memory of the method; the paper was not re-read); the hash is pcg2d after M. Jarzynski and M. Olano, "Hash Functions for GPU Rendering", JCGT 9(3), 2020 (as for P-39); the corner fit, glow and smudge pattern are Margo-own |

| SH13 | variance shadow map: two moments, separable tent blur, Chebyshev upper bound | shadows/src/vsm.ts | Donnelly and Lauritzen 2006, "Variance Shadow Maps", I3D |

Its sections II and VI give the method of Max 1988 ("Horizon mapping: shadows for bump-mapped surfaces", The Visual Computer 4(2)) and the undersampling of one line. Max's own paper is paywalled and was not read |
| SH18 | far penumbra: the share of the sun's disc above a horizon taken as level across the disc, by the area of a circular segment | shadows/src/distance.ts | The fraction rule as Stewart 1996 reports it of Max; the level horizon as Fritsch et al. 2025, "Fast Planetary Shadows using Fourier-Compressed Horizon Maps", HPG talk slides, read in full; segment area is textbook geometry |
| SH19 | shadow style: the light's whole shadow mixed from a floor to 1, floor from intensity and colour | shadows/src/style.ts, shadows/src/shader.ts | Margo-own: Margo design choice. No paper; the only outside inputs are behaviour measured from pixels and a Cycles scene of two suns |
| SH20 | soft shadow rays: rays spread over the sun's solid angle, the lit share being the share that reach it | shadows/src/rt.ts | Cook, Porter and Carpenter 1984, "Distributed Ray Tracing", SIGGRAPH, section 2.3, read in full |
| SH21 | ray against triangle in the walk | bvh/src/gpu.ts, used by shadows/src/rt.ts | Woop, Benthin and Wald 2013, "Watertight Ray/Triangle Intersection", JCGT 2(1), as the bvh package cites it |
| SH22 | hardware ray queries left out, as WebGPU has none | shadows/src/rt.ts | gpuweb pull requests 11054 (acceleration structures) and 11056 (WGSL ray queries), open on 2026-11-07, read as status only |
| SH12 | light pattern: a mask across a directional light | shadows/src/light-function.ts | Segal et al. 1992, "Fast Shadows and Lighting Effects Using Texture Mapping" (projective light textures) |

| S45 | astronomical sky: Julian Day, Delta T, mean and apparent sidereal time, short-series nutation, rigorous precession, the sun's low-accuracy place, the moon's chapter 47 series with parallax and its phase angle and lit fraction, planetary magnitudes; Keplerian planets from tabulated elements with light time and the Earth from the Earth-Moon barycentre; refraction, air mass and extinction; star colour from B-V; the BSC5 fixed-width record layout | sky/src/astro.ts | Meeus, "Astronomical Algorithms", 2nd ed. 1998 (ch. 7, 12, 21, 22, 25, 41, 47, 48); Standish, "Keplerian Elements for Approximate Positions of the Major Planets", JPL Solar System Dynamics (Table 1); Espenak and Meeus, "Polynomial Expressions for Delta T", NASA 2006; Saemundsson, Sky and Telescope 72, 1986 (refraction); Kasten and Young, Applied Optics 28, 1989 (air mass); Ballesteros, EPL 97, 2012 (B-V to temperature); Hoffleit and Warren, BSC5 ReadMe (CDS V/50) |
| S46 | ground albedo feedback: Lambertian ground radiance albedo / pi x E; E the direct sun (20 x T x mu_s) for the first bounce, then the previous order's sky cosine-weighted over 32 upper-hemisphere directions, in a 64-entry table over mu_s; added to the scattering density for directions that end on the ground, times T(ground, up) / T(eye, up); order n reads the ground lit by order n - 2 | sky/src/bruneton.ts | Bruneton & Neyret 2008, "Precomputed Atmospheric Scattering", EGSR, sec. 2 (ground reflection R[L] from the irradiance E) and sec. 4 (per-order algorithm); albedo guide values (fresh snow about 0.8, sand 0.3 to 0.4, grass and forest 0.1 to 0.25) from Coakley 2003, "Reflectance and Albedo, Surface", Encyclopedia of Atmospheric Sciences |
| S47 | planet presets: Mars radius 3389.5 km, 6.36 mb, 214 K, scale height 11 km, Bond albedo 0.25, sunlight 0.431 of Earth's; Rayleigh = CO2 cross section 12.4e-27 cm^2 at 532 nm x (532 / lambda)^4 x p / kT at 680, 550, 440 nm; dust extinction = optical depth / 11 km, grey, single scattering albedo 0.975 / 0.94 / 0.90 (R, G, B), asymmetry 0.67; Mie scattering and absorption per channel in the Hillaire medium | sky/src/presets.ts, sky/src/atmosphere-luts.ts | NASA NSSDCA Mars Fact Sheet (Williams); Sneep & Ubachs 2005, "Direct measurement of the Rayleigh scattering cross section in various gases", JQSRT 92; Wolff et al. 2009, "Wavelength dependence of dust aerosol single scattering albedo as observed by CRISM", JGR 114 E00D04 (red 0.975 as applied by Chen-Chen et al. 2019; green the MGS/TES solar band 0.93 to 0.94 quoted in Wolff et al. 2008, MAMO abstract 9125; blue Margo's reading); Chen-Chen, Perez-Hoyos & Sanchez-Lavega 2019, Icarus 330 (g = 0.673); Lemmon et al. 2015, Icarus 251 (clear-day optical depth) |
| S48 | puddles: wet ground darker by up to 25% and smoother to 35% of its roughness (as `surfaceResponse`); standing water where a three-octave value-noise relief lies below a level read from its measured quantiles, on surfaces within about 15 degrees of flat, with roughness 0.03, no metal and an up normal; rain rings as the slope of a travelling Gaussian bump, one drop per cell of two offset grids | weather/src/puddles.ts, weather/src/scene-patch.ts | Margo-own (wet surfaces as darker, smoother layers after Lagarde 2013, "Water drop 3b: physically based wet surfaces", the published article only) |
| S49 | snow with depth: cover by `snowWeight`'s rule with the snow line jittered by fine value noise; thickness = depth x cover x the same up-facing weight x (1 + variation x (2 x drift noise - 1)), added to the world height of each vertex in the vertex stage; grain as the slope of 8 cm value noise tilting the normal | weather/src/snow.ts | Margo-own |
| S50 | weather regions: signed distance ahead of a moving straight front (dot of position less origin with the direction, less speed x time), smoothstep across its width; cells as circles with a smoothstep falloff and a life ramp of a tenth at each end; states blended component-wise with the wind direction the short way round | weather/src/regions.ts | Margo-own |
| S51 | hail lighting and chips: a sphere impostor's normal from the place across a streak (the quad's world width direction and the view direction), Lambert diffuse plus ambient and a Blinn-Phong glint of exponent 40; chips as ballistic paths from the first landing point, launched at random angles and speeds, fading over 0.35 s | weather/src/hail.ts | Margo-own (Blinn 1977, "Models of light reflection for computer synthesized pictures", SIGGRAPH) |
| S52 | weather drive: cloud cover = state cloud; layer extinction 0.02 + 0.06 x the strongest of rain, snow, hail and lightning; drift = ground wind x 3; fog: exponential extinction sigma = `fogDensity`, and for a squared falloff k = sigma / sqrt(3) so both fall to 5 percent contrast at 3 / sigma (Koschmieder) | weather/src/drive.ts | Margo-own |
| S53 | weather audio: rain gain = volume x (rain + hail / 2)^0.7 through a low-pass from 2.5 to 8 kHz with hail; wind gain = volume x (speed / 25)^1.5 x (1 + gust / 3), capped at 2 x volume, through a band-pass at 250 + 30 x speed Hz; noise from a 32-bit linear congruential generator, brown noise by a leaky integrator made periodic by removing its end-to-end drift; thunder as brown noise with a 20 ms attack, a crack over the first 0.15 s and an exp(-3 t / length) fall | weather/src/audio.ts | Margo-own |
| S54 | METAR and SPECI: groups of WMO-No. 306 Manual on Codes Vol. I.1 FM 15 and FM 16 and ICAO Annex 3 (identifier, DDHHMMZ, AUTO/COR/NIL, dddff(f)Gfmfm KT/MPS/KMH and dndndnVdxdxdx, VVVV and CAVOK, RVR, w'w' intensity, descriptor and phenomena, NsNsNshshshs with CB/TCU, VV, NSC/NCD/SKC/CLR, T'T'/T'dT'd with M, QPHPHPHPH, REw'w', WS, sea groups, trend) and the United States FMH-1 variant (statute miles with fractions, APHPHPHPH in inches of mercury, RMK); fog from visibility by Koschmieder's law, extinction = 3 / meteorological optical range (5 percent contrast) | weather/src/data.ts | WMO-No. 306, Manual on Codes, Vol. I.1 Part A; ICAO Annex 3, Meteorological Service for International Air Navigation; Federal Meteorological Handbook No. 1 (FMH-1), Surface Weather Observations and Reports; Koschmieder 1924, "Theorie der horizontalen Sichtweite" |
| S55 | day presets: sun direction from azimuth and elevation (Sky's `sunDirectionFromAngles`), the sun's colour and strength from the sky's own transmittance (`sunlight`); noon 70 degrees, golden hour 5 degrees (the sun below about 6 degrees), dawn 1 degree; overcast as the CIE standard overcast sky (no direct sun, an even diffuse sky), its ground light about a fifth of clear noon's | scenery/src/presets.ts | CIE S 011/E:2003 (ISO 15469:2004), Spatial distribution of daylight, CIE standard general sky (type 1, overcast); daylight horizontal illuminance of overcast and clear skies as in Darula and Kittler 2002, "CIE general sky standard defining luminance distributions", Proc. eSim 2002 |
| S56 | scene presets: fog density = 3.912 / visibility (the 2% contrast threshold), visibility 20 km, 10 km, 2 km and 600 m; exposure in stops to bring each preset's ground back toward noon's; sky fog, Bloom and Tonemap from their packages | scenery/src/scenes.ts | Koschmieder 1924, "Theorie der horizontalen Sichtweite", Beitr. Phys. freien Atmos. 12; WMO-No. 8 (Guide to Instruments and Methods of Observation), meteorological optical range |
| S57 | Milky Way: the ICRS (J2000) to galactic rotation matrix; the band's surface brightness in V magnitudes per square arcsecond scaled by a magnitude-0 star's brightness and the pixel's solid angle; a Margo shape model of the disc, bulge and Great Rift; air mass for extinction | scenery/src/stars.ts | ESA 1997, The Hipparcos and Tycho Catalogues, ESA SP-1200, Vol. 1, section 1.5.3; Mellinger 2009, "A Color All-Sky Panorama Image of the Milky Way", PASP 121, 1180; Leinert et al. 1998, "The 1997 reference of diffuse night sky brightness", A&AS 127, 1; Kasten and Young 1989, Applied Optics 28, 4735 |
| S58 | aurora: the oxygen 557.7 nm (O 1S, 0.74 s) and 630.0 nm (O 1D, about 110 s, quenched below about 150 to 200 km) lines and the N2+ 427.8 nm band, Chapman-shaped volume emission peaking near 110, 230 and 105 km, curtains along arcs running magnetic east-west with folds and rays; intensities in rayleighs on the International Brightness Coefficient scale; line colours through the CIE 1931 colour-matching functions to linear sRGB; luminance to V magnitudes per square arcsecond by 10.8e4 x 10^(-0.4 S) cd/m2 | scenery/src/aurora.ts | Chamberlain 1961, Physics of the Aurora and Airglow (Academic Press); Omholt 1971, The Optical Aurora (Springer); Rees 1989, Physics and Chemistry of the Upper Atmosphere (Cambridge); Hunten, Roach and Chamberlain 1956, "A photometric unit for the airglow and aurora", J. Atmos. Terr. Phys. 8, 345 (the rayleigh); CIE 015:2004 Colorimetry; IEC 61966-2-1 (sRGB) |
| S59 | terrain queries: bilinear height and central-difference normals as the clipmap's vertex shader; an implicit min-max quadtree over the cells (maximum mipmap) walked nearest child first; exact ray against each cell's bilinear patch (height along the ray quadratic in t, solved in the cell's own parameter); Terrarium elevation decoding | terrain/src/query.ts | Tevs, Ihrke and Seidel 2008, "Maximum Mipmaps for Fast, Accurate, and Scalable Dynamic Height Field Rendering", I3D; Ramsey, Potter and Hansen 2004, "Ray Bilinear Patch Intersections", Journal of Graphics Tools 9(3); Amanatides and Woo 1987, "A Fast Voxel Traversal Algorithm for Ray Tracing", Eurographics (the nearest-first column order); Mapzen Terrain Tiles documentation (Terrarium format) |
| S60 | terrain layer materials: triplanar tangent-space normals turned into a volume gradient per projection, blended by the triplanar weights and applied as a surface gradient; one-pass height blending of splat weights; glTF ORM channel order for occlusion and roughness | terrain/src/layers-pbr.ts | Mikkelsen 2020, "Surface Gradient-Based Bump Mapping Framework", Journal of Computer Graphics Techniques 9(3); Mikkelsen 2010, "Bump Mapping Unparametrized Surfaces on the GPU", JGT 15(1); Mishkinis 2013, "Advanced Terrain Texture Splatting" (Gamasutra); Geiss 2007, GPU Gems 3 ch. 1 (triplanar weights); Khronos glTF 2.0 specification (metallicRoughness and occlusion channels) |
| S61 | terrain height streaming: a heightmap window of tiles that moves with the camera by whole tiles, updating only the exposed tiles (the clipmap's incremental update), kept texels scrolled by GPU texture copies through a scratch texture instead of toroidal addressing; nearest-first loading and banded uploads within a texel budget per frame | terrain/src/stream.ts | Tanner, Migdal and Jones 1998, "The Clipmap: A Virtual Mipmap", SIGGRAPH; Losasso and Hoppe 2004, "Geometry Clipmaps: Terrain Rendering Using Nested Regular Grids", SIGGRAPH, section 3; Asirvatham and Hoppe 2005, "Terrain Rendering Using GPU-Based Geometry Clipmaps", GPU Gems 2 ch. 2; W3C WebGPU specification (copyTextureToTexture, writeTexture) |
| S62 | terrain sculpt and paint brushes: compute-pass dabs (raise, lower, binomial smoothing, flatten, weight paint) over a copied rectangle, Wendland C2 falloff, stroke undo by rectangles of before and after values | terrain/src/sculpt.ts | Wendland 1995, "Piecewise polynomial, positive definite and compactly supported radial functions of minimal degree", Advances in Computational Mathematics 4; Burt and Adelson 1983, "The Laplacian Pyramid as a Compact Image Code", IEEE Transactions on Communications 31(4) (binomial filter); Gamma, Helm, Johnson and Vlissides 1994, "Design Patterns", Memento and Command; W3C WebGPU and WGSL specifications (storage textures, copyTextureToTexture) |
| S63 | terrain holes: a cell mask over the heightmap's bounds tested per fragment in every terrain pass (colour, SSR capture, shadow, occluder), shapes rasterised by cell centre (even-odd polygons), queries that skip hole cells | terrain/src/holes.ts, terrain/src/index.ts, terrain/src/shader.ts, terrain/src/query.ts | Shimrat 1962, "Algorithm 112: Position of point relative to polygon", Communications of the ACM 5(8) (even-odd crossing test); W3C WGSL specification (discard); point sampling at cell centres as in any rasteriser (Pineda 1988, "A Parallel Algorithm for Polygon Rasterization", SIGGRAPH) |
| S64 | terrain runtime virtual texture: clipmap levels of one texel size each twice as coarse, toroidal addressing so a move bakes only the exposed rows and columns, ground baked top down from the terrain's own colour function per texel, decals and road segments blended on in order, level chosen per pixel from footprint and distance and blended with the next | terrain/src/rvt.ts, terrain/src/index.ts | Tanner, Migdal and Jones 1998, "The Clipmap: A Virtual Mipmap", SIGGRAPH (levels and toroidal update, section 4); Widmark 2012, "Terrain in Battlefield 3: A Modern, Complete and Scalable System", GDC, and van Waveren 2009, "id Tech 5 Challenges: From Texture Virtualization to Massive Parallelization", SIGGRAPH course (procedural virtual texture baked from the terrain's layers); Losasso and Hoppe 2004, "Geometry Clipmaps", SIGGRAPH, section 2.4 (blending between levels) |
| S65 | terrain near displacement: parallax occlusion by a linear search with fewer steps facing the eye and a secant step, offset limiting at grazing angles, the hit shaded by the terrain's own layer loop with the surface's derivatives; self-shadow by a ray through a maximum mipmap of the layers' height maps (each texel the highest of a square, built by a compute pass), passing over squares under the ray, testing the blended bilinear relief at single texels, softened over a small angle at the horizon | terrain/src/displace.ts, terrain/src/layers-pbr.ts | Tatarchuk 2006, "Dynamic Parallax Occlusion Mapping with Approximate Soft Shadows", I3D; Policarpo, Oliveira and Comba 2005, "Real-Time Relief Mapping on Arbitrary Polygonal Surfaces", I3D; Welsh 2004, "Parallax Mapping with Offset Limiting"; Tevs, Ihrke and Seidel 2008, "Maximum Mipmaps for Fast, Accurate, and Scalable Dynamic Height Field Rendering", I3D |
| S66 | terrain deformation: a camera-centred deformation map addressed toroidally, rows and columns cleared as the window moves; prints pressed by a compute pass over each print's box (ellipse and capsule profiles with a smooth wall and a raised rim), the deepest kept per texel by an atomic maximum over float bits; refill by a fixed depth a second; the lit shader marches the view ray through the relief (parallax occlusion, offset limited) and shades the hit with the relief's slope as normal | terrain/src/deform.ts | Tanner, Migdal and Jones 1998, "The Clipmap: A Virtual Mipmap" (SIGGRAPH); Barré-Brisebois 2014, "Deformable Snow Rendering in Batman: Arkham Origins" (GDC talk); Michels and Sikachev 2016, "Deferred Snow Deformation in Rise of the Tomb Raider" (GPU Pro 7); Tatarchuk 2006, "Dynamic Parallax Occlusion Mapping with Approximate Soft Shadows" (I3D); Welsh 2004, "Parallax Mapping with Offset Limiting" |
| S67 | mesh to terrain blending: per pixel of a mesh, its height above the heightmap's bilinear ground (as the terrain's vertex shader reads it) moved by lattice value noise; a smoothstep fade over `height` mixes the mesh's colour toward the terrain's runtime virtual texture at the ground point under the pixel (read at the pixel's footprint), its roughness to the terrain's and its normal toward the heightmap's central-difference normal; per-mesh settings in a storage buffer indexed by mesh id | terrain/src/blend.ts, terrain/src/mesh-patch.ts | van Waveren 2009, "id Tech 5 Challenges: From Texture Virtualization to Massive Parallelization" (SIGGRAPH course); Chen 2015, "Adaptive Virtual Texture Rendering in Far Cry 4" (GDC talk); Wellons 2018, "Prospecting for Hash Functions"; the fade itself by method |
| S68 | planet terrain: six cube faces, each a quadtree of square patches mapped to the sphere by the equal-angle warp tan(s pi / 4) then normalised; a patch splits while its vertex spacing on screen exceeds a pixel bound, with skirts hung from patch edges; horizon culling against the cone and tangent plane of a sphere under the lowest ground; frustum planes from the rows of the view-projection matrix; patches drawn relative to the eye from float64 centres; fractal value noise ground with octaves cut at the vertex spacing | terrain/src/planet.ts | Zucker and Higashi 2018, "Cube-to-sphere Projections for Procedural Texturing and Beyond" (JCGT 7(2)); Ulrich 2002, "Rendering Massive Terrains using Chunked Level of Detail Control" (SIGGRAPH course); Cozzi and Ring 2011, "3D Engine Design for Virtual Globes" (chapters 5 and 12); Gribb and Hartmann 2001, "Fast Extraction of Viewing Frustum Planes from the World-View-Projection Matrix"; Ebert, Musgrave, Peachey, Perlin and Worley 2003, "Texturing and Modeling: A Procedural Approach" (chapter 16); Wellons 2018, "Prospecting for Hash Functions" |
| S69 | world-space UI panels: the UI's draw list painted into an sRGB texture by the screen renderer's own instanced pipeline, mipmapped, then sampled on a quad placed by a rigid pose; ray and plane intersection by the plane's normal and the pose's in-plane axes, pixel coordinates from the hit's projections onto those axes; a screen ray from the camera position through the unprojected pixel; an XR pointing ray from the target-ray pose's -Z axis as the WebXR Device API specification defines target ray space | ui/src/world.ts, ui/src/painter.ts | W3C WebXR Device API (XRInputSource targetRaySpace points along its -Z axis); Shirley and Marschner, Fundamentals of Computer Graphics (ray and plane intersection); W3C WebGPU specification (render attachments, mipmap sampling) |
| S70 | wrapping flex rows: items collected into lines in order while their outer base sizes and the gaps fit the row's inner width (an item wider than the row alone on its line), then grow and shrink resolved per line, each line's cross size its tallest item, lines stacked by the row gap and placed by justify and align within their line | ui/src/layout.ts | W3C CSS Flexible Box Layout Module Level 1, section 9.3 "Main Size Determination" (collect flex items into flex lines) and section 9.4 "Cross Size Determination" (cross size of each flex line) |
| S71 | UI text direction: a text's direction is that of its first strong character (bidi class R or AL right to left, L left to right), left to right without one, and `'start'` alignment is the right edge of a right-to-left paragraph | ui/src/ui.ts | Unicode Standard Annex #9, Unicode Bidirectional Algorithm, rules P2 and P3 (paragraph embedding level from the first strong character); W3C CSS Text Module Level 3, section 7.1 (`text-align: start` is the start edge of the line box's inline direction) |
| S72 | UI drawing on WebGL2: instanced quads with per-instance attributes (`vertexAttribDivisor` 1), each run drawn with its attribute offsets moved to its first instance, glyph atlases as 2D array textures written with `texSubImage3D`, clip regions as scissor boxes in bottom-up window rows | ui/src/gl.ts | Khronos OpenGL ES 3.0.6 specification, sections 2.9.3 (instanced arrays and the divisor), 3.8.3 and 3.8.5 (array textures, `TexStorage3D`, `TexSubImage3D`) and 4.1.2 (scissor test in window coordinates); Khronos WebGL 2.0 specification, section 3.7 (`UNPACK_FLIP_Y_WEBGL`, `UNPACK_PREMULTIPLY_ALPHA_WEBGL`) |
| S74 | virtual lists: of n rows of height h and gap g in a view of height V scrolled by s, build rows floor(s / (h + g)) - k to ceil((s + V) / (h + g)) + k (k the overscan), and size two spacers to the rows before and after so the content height is n h + (n - 1) g | ui/src/virtual.ts | Margo-own: the arithmetic follows from rows of one height; W3C WAI-ARIA 1.2, `listbox` and `option` roles, and `aria-setsize` and `aria-posinset` for a list whose rows are not all in the DOM |
| S75 | UI markup and style sheets: elements, attributes in quotes, comments, processing instructions skipped, the five predefined entities and decimal and hexadecimal character references; type, id and class selectors with descendant and child combinators, specificity counted as (ids, classes, types) and compared in that order, later rules winning ties | ui/src/markup.ts | W3C Extensible Markup Language (XML) 1.0, Fifth Edition, sections 2.4 (character data), 2.5 (comments), 3.1 (start tags, end tags, attributes) and 4.1 (character and entity references) and 4.6 (predefined entities); W3C Selectors Level 3, sections 6.1, 6.5, 6.6, 8.1, 8.2 and 9 (specificity); W3C CSS Cascading and Inheritance Level 4, section 6.4 (order of appearance) |
| S73 | biome settlements: village sites scored by flatness, nearness to water and lowness; roads as least-cost grid paths whose step cost grows with the grade, with river cells priced as bridges and built roads cheaper to reuse; a network of a minimum spanning tree plus links where the tree's detour is long; A* with a straight-distance bound; Douglas-Peucker thinning; houses on lots facing their street | biomes/src/settlements.ts | Galin, Peytavie, Marechal and Guerin, "Procedural Generation of Roads", Computer Graphics Forum (Eurographics) 2010; Galin, Peytavie, Guerin and Benes, "Authoring Hierarchical Road Networks", Computer Graphics Forum (Pacific Graphics) 2011; Hart, Nilsson and Raphael, IEEE Transactions on Systems Science and Cybernetics, 1968; Prim, Bell System Technical Journal, 1957; Douglas and Peucker, The Canadian Cartographer, 1973; Borgefors, "Distance transformations in digital images", CVGIP 1986; Parish and Muller, "Procedural Modeling of Cities", SIGGRAPH 2001 |
| S76 | label decluttering: point-feature labels taken greedily in rank order, each tried at a fixed list of candidate positions around its point (above right preferred, then the other corners, then the sides) and kept at the first that overlaps no label already placed, else dropped; placed boxes bucketed in a uniform grid so each test reads only neighbouring cells | labels/src/declutter.ts | Christensen, Marks and Shieber, "An Empirical Study of Algorithms for Point-Feature Label Placement", ACM Transactions on Graphics 14(3), 1995 (greedy placement, the four- and eight-position candidate models); Imhof, "Positioning Names on Maps", The American Cartographer 2(2), 1975 (preference for the upper right position); Margo-own: the grid buckets and the tie order |
