Cook, "Rendering Antialiased Shadows with Depth Maps", SIGGRAPH 1987 (percentage-closer filtering); W3C WebGPU and WGSL specifications (`textureLoad` of a depth texture); Margo-own: the bounded-rate fade and the stale-result rule |
| S83 | DOM anchor occlusion on WebGL2: a one-pixel point drawn after the scene at the anchor's depth inside an any-samples-passed occlusion query, colour and depth writes off; results read when available, one round in flight per anchor | html/src/index.ts | Khronos WebGL 2.0 Specification (queries, `ANY_SAMPLES_PASSED`, results never available in the issuing frame); Khronos OpenGL ES 3.0 Specification, section 4.1.6 occlusion queries; Margo-own: the per-anchor round and the stale-result rule shared with WebGPU |
| S85 | DOM drawn on a scene quad: the element rasterized into a texture (HTML-in-canvas drawElementImage in the canvas paint event, or the element tree with computed styles inlined into an SVG foreignObject image drawn into a 2D canvas), uploaded with copyExternalImageToTexture and drawn depth-tested; the pointer point solved on the quad's plane through the plane-to-clip homography (columns x, y and w of view-projection times model, two linear equations), and the element's invisible holder translated so the hit UV lies under the pointer | html/src/texture.ts | WICG HTML-in-Canvas explainer (`content="drawable"`, `drawable`, `drawElementImage`, the paint event, `requestPaint`); W3C SVG 2, the `foreignObject` element; W3C WebGPU, `copyExternalImageToTexture` and its origin-clean rule; W3C CSSOM View Module, `elementFromPoint`; Margo-own: the holder routing and the redraw triggers |
| S86 | growable DOM anchor slots: an array of slots doubled to the capacity when full (amortised constant time a slot), freed slots kept in a list and the lowest taken first, GPU buffers re-made at the size between depth reads and the per-frame work bounded by the highest slot in use | html/src/index.ts | T. H. Cormen, C. E. Leiserson, R. L. Rivest, C. Stein, *Introduction to Algorithms*, dynamic tables (table doubling and its amortised cost); W3C WebGPU (`writeBuffer` ranges, `getMappedRange` offsets and sizes); Margo-own: regrowing only while no read is in flight |
| S77 | biome placement graphs: a JSON data-flow graph of density rules evaluated in topological order at each candidate; range nodes as Wendland bands like suitability, value noise, products, minimum, maximum, capped sums, differences, inversion, piecewise linear curves and linear blends, all on Q16.16 integers; the factor thins candidates by density | biomes/src/graph.ts, biomes/src/graph-hook.ts | A. B. Kahn, "Topological sorting of large networks", Communications of the ACM 5(11), 1962; B. Lane and P. Prusinkiewicz, "Generating Spatial Distributions for Multilevel Models of Plant Communities", Graphics Interface 2002 (density thinning); H. Wendland, "Piecewise polynomial, positive definite and compactly supported radial functions of minimal degree", Advances in Computational Mathematics 4, 1995; R. M. Smelik, T. Tutenel, R. Bidarra and B. Benes, "A Survey on Procedural Modelling for Virtual Worlds", Computer Graphics Forum 33(6), 2014 (rule-based placement by artists). No other engine's graph or placement code was read |
| S80 | biome ecosystem growth: an individual-based forest gap model on integers; logistic crown growth scaled by light, shading by the overlap of taller crowns only (size-asymmetric competition), base, senescence and suppression mortality, seedlings by crown area dispersed by a 2Dt kernel, standing dead snags | biomes/src/ecosystem.ts | D. B. Botkin, J. F. Janak and J. R. Wallis, "Some Ecological Consequences of a Computer Model of Forest Growth", Journal of Ecology 60(3), 1972; S. W. Pacala, C. D. Canham, J. Saponara, J. A. Silander, R. K. Kobe and E. Ribbens, "Forest Models Defined by Field Measurements: Estimation, Error Analysis and Dynamics", Ecological Monographs 66(1), 1996; J. S. Clark, M. Silman, R. Kern, E. Macklin and J. HilleRisLambers, "Seed Dispersal Near and Far: Patterns across Temperate and Tropical Forests", Ecology 80(5), 1999; J. Weiner, "Asymmetric competition in plant populations", Trends in Ecology and Evolution 5(11), 1990; I. E. Bella, "A New Competition Model for Individual Trees", Forest Science 17(3), 1971. No other engine's or simulator's code was read |
| S84 | scatter instance picking: a uniform XZ grid of instance world boxes walked by a 2D digital differential analyser; the ray in each instance's local space (inverse of its affine matrix) against its box by slabs and its triangles by the Moller-Trumbore test; world boxes from the absolute 3x3 | scatter/src/pick.ts | J. Amanatides and A. Woo, "A Fast Voxel Traversal Algorithm for Ray Tracing", Eurographics 1987; T. L. Kay and J. T. Kajiya, "Ray Tracing Complex Scenes", SIGGRAPH 1986; T. Moller and B. Trumbore, "Fast, Minimum Storage Ray-Triangle Intersection", Journal of Graphics Tools 2(1), 1997; J. Arvo, "Transforming Axis-Aligned Bounding Boxes", Graphics Gems, 1990 |
| S88 | scatter trampling: a push field of one 2D vector a cell around the camera, shifted in whole cells, decayed by exp(-3 dt / recover), stamped with the stronger of the old push and strength * (1 - (d / r)^2) away from each interactor; each instance turned about its root by the axis-angle (Rodrigues) rotation that tips +Y toward the push | scatter/src/interact.ts | O. Rodrigues, "Des lois geometriques qui regissent les deplacements d'un systeme solide dans l'espace", Journal de Mathematiques Pures et Appliquees 5, 1840 |
| S87 | package object editor: objects found by their documented shapes (a plugin's `list`, `options`, a PBR record's packed factor arrays), own writable fields and class accessors with a setter read through property descriptors, values checked against documented ranges and recorded as undo steps that restore an unset option by deleting it; colour inputs converted between sRGB and linear with the IEC 61966-2-1 transfer function | inspector/src/packages.ts, inspector/src/color.ts | ECMAScript (Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors, prototype chain); IEC 61966-2-1 sRGB; the packages' own READMEs and option docs; Margo-own: shape discovery and unset-on-undo |

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SP1 | view-dependent colour from real SH degree 0-3, +0.5 DC offset | splats/src/shader.ts | real SH normalisation: Sloan 2008, "Stupid Spherical Harmonics Tricks" App. A. Sign pattern and +0.5 offset are the 3DGS asset convention (Kerbl et al. 2023), needed to read trained .ply files |
| SP2 | EWA projection (view rotation, perspective Jacobian), 2D low-pass dilation, eigen axes, 3 sigma extent | splats/src/shader.ts | Zwicker et al. 2001, EWA Splatting eq. 29-31; 0.3 px^2 dilation is the 3D Gaussian Splatting asset convention (Kerbl et al. 2023) |
| SP3 | Gaussian falloff, alpha cap, low-alpha discard | splats/src/shader.ts | Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting", App. C (clamp to 0.99, skip below 1/255) |
| SP4 | alpha-tested splat depth: the colour pass's 3 sigma quad and Gaussian falloff, depth written where opacity reaches a threshold, nearest wins (reversed Z) | splats/src/depth.ts | Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering", ACM TOG 42(4), App. C (falloff, 0.99 cap); Zwicker et al. 2001, "EWA Splatting" (ellipses); W3C WebGPU specification (depth-only render pass, depthCompare) |
| SP5 | splat level of detail: octree of splat centres, inner nodes moment-matched on a grid, cut refined by projected geometric error largest first within a budget, parent kept until all children load | splats/src/lod.ts | Luebke et al. 2003, "Level of Detail for 3D Graphics", ch. 4 (screen-space error); OGC 3D Tiles 1.1 (geometricError, REPLACE refinement); Kerbl et al. 2024, "A Hierarchical 3D Gaussian Representation for Real-Time Rendering of Very Large Datasets", ACM TOG 43(4) |
| SP6 | one global splat order: every cloud written into ranges of one pooled cloud (first-fit free list, merged on release) so one depth sort orders all splats | splats/src/set.ts | Porter and Duff 1984, "Compositing Digital Images", SIGGRAPH (back-to-front over is order dependent); Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering", ACM TOG 42(4) (one global depth sort) |
| SP7 | splat relighting and shadow casting: normal from the smallest covariance eigenvector (closed-form symmetric 3x3 eigenvalue), core diffuse terms; EWA ellipse in light clip space, stochastic per-texel coverage | splats/src/lighting.ts | Jiang et al. 2024, "GaussianShader", CVPR; Gao et al. 2023, "Relightable 3D Gaussian"; Smith 1961, "Eigenvalues of a symmetric 3x3 matrix", CACM 4(4); Zwicker et al. 2001, "EWA Splatting"; Enderton et al. 2010, "Stochastic Transparency", I3D; Jarzynski and Olano 2020, "Hash Functions for GPU Rendering", JCGT |
| SP8 | GPU splat picking and brush editing: one compute pass per operation over the projected ellipses; nearest pick by atomic min over an order-preserving depth key, then over indices; SH paint sets the DC term and fades the higher bands | splats/src/brush.ts | Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering", ACM TOG 42(4), App. C (falloff, 0.99 cap); Sloan 2008, "Stupid Spherical Harmonic Tricks", App. A (SH basis); W3C WGSL specification (atomicMin, bitcast) |
| SP9 | SPZ and SOG writers: SPZ version 3 (24-bit fixed point, byte attributes, smallest-three 10-bit rotation, gzip); SOG version 2 (log-transformed 16-bit means, 1D k-means codebooks, SH palette by principal-axis grouping, stored ZIP); lossless WebP writer (literal pixels, per-channel canonical prefix codes, length-limited) | splats/src/encode.ts, splats/src/webp.ts | Niantic Spatial SPZ format documentation; PlayCanvas SOG format documentation (version 2); RFC 9649, WebP Image Format (VP8L lossless bitstream); PKWARE APPNOTE.TXT (ZIP stored entries); ISO 3309 CRC-32; Lloyd 1982, "Least squares quantization in PCM", IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory 28(2) |
| SP10 | animated splats: 4D sequence by linear mix of two frames per splat; linear blend skinning of centres and of covariances by the blended linear part (A S A^T) | splats/src/animate.ts | Magnenat-Thalmann et al. 1988, "Joint-dependent local deformations for hand animation and object grasping", Graphics Interface (linear blend skinning); Zwicker et al. 2001, "EWA Splatting" (a Gaussian under a linear map); Wu et al. 2024, "4D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Dynamic Scene Rendering", CVPR (per-frame Gaussian attributes) |
| SP11 | anti-aliased splats: opacity scaled by sqrt(det(S) / det(S + 0.3 I)) of the screen covariance, the 2D Mip filter with the 0.3 px^2 dilation | splats/src/shader.ts | Yu et al. 2024, "Mip-Splatting: Alias-free 3D Gaussian Splatting", CVPR, eq. 9 (2D Mip filter); Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting", App. C (0.3 px^2 dilation) |
| SP12 | splat picking with meshes: the colour pass's projection run again in a vertex shader for a 3 x 3 pick tile under a cropped view-projection, IDs written where the Gaussian opacity reaches a threshold, nearest centre wins against the meshes' depth (reversed Z) | splats/src/pick.ts | Kerbl et al. 2023, "3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering", ACM TOG 42(4), App. C (falloff, 0.99 cap); Zwicker et al. 2001, "EWA Splatting" (ellipses); W3C WebGPU specification (r32uint render target, instanced triangle strips, depthCompare) |

The batch 168 timing drives `@mkkellogg/gaussian-splats-3d` 0.4.7 as a black box from its README (https://github.com/mkkellogg/GaussianSplats3D): `Viewer` with `selfDrivenMode: false`, then `update` and `render`. The library source was not opened. The photograph and the camera row are the published truck test image `000001` and `cameras.json` from the 3D Gaussian Splatting dataset (Kerbl et al. 2023). The pose is COLMAP's camera frame (https://colmap.github.io/format.html): x right, y down, z forward. Both interpretations of the stored rotation were scored. The shared-memory GPU sort drew nothing in this page, so the timed rival uses the README's CPU sort (`gpuAcceleratedSort` and `sharedMemoryForWorkers` false).

| UZ1 | USD composition: layer stacks, list edits, arc strength, implied classes, variant selection, path mapping, time offsets | usdz/src/stage.ts, usdz/src/layer.ts | **Written basis:** AOUSD USD Core Specification v1.1.0 (e8f44c0, 2026-08-13), Composition chapter: Composition Arcs, Inherits, Specializes, Strength Ordering, Namespace Mappings, Time Codes Per Second, published at github.com/aousd/specifications-public under CC-BY-ND-4.0 (read, cited, not copied into this repository); v1.0.1 compared; the USD Glossary at openusd.org (Index, Inherits, Specializes, LIVERPS Strength Ordering). **Observed only, the specification does not settle them:** two rules marked in stage.ts, taken from running OpenUSD 26.08 on our own stages and reading its public `GetPrimStack`, `GetPropertyStack` and prim index dump output: a site an authored arc reaches gives no opinions through an implied arc; and specializes arcs order by the depth of the stage prim they first reach, a repeated class site counting once through the later arc. **History, not hidden:** OpenUSD's source was never opened. Job 1 of batch 44 was debugged using remembered OpenUSD internals as hypotheses (inert implied nodes, origin-based arc order), then confirmed by black-box runs. Memory is not a source, so both were taken out on 2026-11-07 and the code rebuilt from the documents above. The sample implementations in aousd/core-spec-supplemental-public are code and were not read. | VX27 | shared editing: a last-writer-wins register per cell stamped by a Lamport clock with the site name as tie-break (a state-based CRDT applied as operations), plus a per-site sequence for catch-up | voxel-edit/src/collab.ts | Margo-own; after L. Lamport, "Time, clocks, and the ordering of events in a distributed system", CACM 1978, and M. Shapiro et al., "Conflict-free replicated data types", SSS 2011 |
| VX28 | voxel-net entities: server-stepped bodies and brains, the area of interest as a circle with a larger leave circle (hysteresis) found through a hashed grid, nearest first past a cap, `EntityCodec` deltas against the last message sent | voxel-net/src/entities.ts | Margo-own; after J.-S. Boulanger, J. Kienzle and C. Verbrugge, "Comparing interest management algorithms for massively multiplayer games", NetGames 2006 (section 3.1), and G. Fiedler, "State Synchronization" (2015) |
| VX29 | voxel-net shards: square regions of the x-z plane given to shards by a hash or your rule, a handoff past a margin (hysteresis), HMAC-SHA-256 signed one-time tickets that carry the player to the next shard | voxel-net/src/shards.ts | Margo-own; after H. Krawczyk, M. Bellare and R. Canetti, RFC 2104 "HMAC: Keyed-Hashing for Message Authentication" (1997) and NIST FIPS 198-1, through the W3C Web Cryptography API; the region hash is the 32-bit finalizer described for MurmurHash3 by A. Appleby (public description); zoned servers with handoff as described in J. Smed, T. Kaukoranta and H. Hakonen, "Aspects of networking in multiplayer computer games" (The Electronic Library 20(2), 2002) |
| VX30 | voxel-net WebTransport: inputs as datagrams with copies of the last few, the server cutting repeated steps and filling a lost gap with the last keys held, whole player snapshots with late ones dropped | voxel-net/src/webtransport.ts, server.ts, client.ts | Margo-own; after G. Fiedler, "What every programmer needs to know about game networking" and "Reliability and congestion avoidance over UDP" (gafferongames.com, 2010 and 2008), on the W3C WebTransport API (Candidate Recommendation) through @margoengine/netcode |

## Extruded 3D text: `@margoengine/text/extrude`

Written and repaired clean room from the specifications and papers below. No other engine or font-library source
was read, adapted or copied. Only the two standard font assets and their licence were downloaded (see
[third-party notices](../THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md)). The private CAD repository's package/API boundaries were inspected;
its CAD APIs do not own this optional font decoder or text mesher, and no kernel code/dependency is added here.

| Area | Source and implementation |
| --- | --- |
| Bounded SFNT, cmap, TrueType outlines and composite transforms | [Microsoft OpenType](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/typography/opentype/spec/), especially [cmap](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/typography/opentype/spec/cmap) and [glyf](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/typography/opentype/spec/glyf). Component attachment uses raw point indices before implied points and curve flattening. Scaled/unscaled offsets follow the component flags; the default unscaled offset matches the Microsoft/Apple recommended default. |
| CFF INDEX/DICT/charset and Type 2 | Adobe [Technical Note 5176](https://adobe-type-tools.github.io/font-tech-notes/pdfs/5176.CFF.pdf) and [Technical Note 5177](https://adobe-type-tools.github.io/font-tech-notes/pdfs/5177.Type2.pdf). The interpreter shares its stack, width state, hints and transient storage across calls; masks consume bytes, all curve/flex families are explicit, and recursion/operand bounds are enforced. Appendix B/C numerical StandardEncoding/charset data comes from 5176. Deprecated endchar composites resolve by SID; nested composites are forbidden by 5177 Appendix C. FontMatrix is applied once after decoding raw coordinates. |
| Polygon filling and triangulation | Nonzero winding is computed on split contour edges; only edges separating zero/nonzero faces survive, oriented with fill on the left. Our arrangement splitting, relative coordinate welding and stitching are derived here from line intersection and winding-number definitions. [Eberly, Triangulation by Ear Clipping](https://www.geometrictools.com/Documentation/TriangulationByEarClipping.pdf), sections 2-5: duplicate bridge endpoints, mutually visible hole connections and ear removal. Visibility here is tested against every edge and the local interior cone, rather than a nearest-vertex guess. Incomplete clipping throws, and cap area is verified. |
| Bevel extrusion | Parallel-edge offset intersections give miter displacements; sine/cosine profiles give rounded rims, one segment a chamfer. Caps consume the exact terminal wall rings; wall normals come from their cross products and are normalized. Offset-ring crossings, reversed edges and collapses are rejected. |
| Independent outline evidence | Our `generate-outline-reference.py` uses the public Windows [GetGlyphOutlineW](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/wingdi/nf-wingdi-getglyphoutlinew), [TTPOLYGONHEADER](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/wingdi/ns-wingdi-ttpolygonheader) and [TTPOLYCURVE](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/wingdi/ns-wingdi-ttpolycurve) contracts, checks the selected face's head bytes, and freezes unhinted absolute cubic controls. Our separate de Casteljau traversal compares both directions with the analytic chord-error bound max second derivative / (8 n squared), plus 16.16 control quantization. No decoder logic is shared with production. |

## GPU pass timing: `@margoengine/stats` (`GPUProfiler`)

Written from the specification and API documentation below; no other profiler's code was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Which pass timestamps are time going backwards | W3C WebGPU, section 20.4 "Timestamp Query" (Candidate Recommendation Draft, read 2026-11-08): "Timestamp values are implementation-defined. Applications must handle arbitrary timestamp results, and should not be written in such a way that unexpected timestamps cause an application failure." Its note: the device "may reset the timestamp counter occasionally", giving "negative deltas", and "these data points can safely be discarded". Nothing in the section orders one pass's timestamps against another's, so a pass that starts before the pass recorded ahead of it is accepted when the two overlap; an end before its own start, or a pass that ends before the previous pass starts, is discarded as a reset |
| Why passes overlap on Apple GPUs | Apple Metal documentation, "Sampling GPU data into counter sample buffers" (read 2026-11-08): Apple silicon samples "at the stage boundary"; the counters are sampled "when the GPU starts or finishes a stage", and only a sample with a barrier "waits for the GPU to complete all the previous commands in the buffer before sampling". How Chrome maps WebGPU timestamps onto these samples was not read (no browser source) |

## Data track: `@margoengine/compute`

Written clean room in October 2026 from the specifications, papers and API documentation below. No source code of
another GPU, array or data library (NumPy, CuPy, TensorFlow.js, GPU.js, taichi.js, Random123, cuFFT/VkFFT and the like)
was read. API names and semantics follow the libraries' public documentation so data scientists find what they
expect; the documentation describes behaviour, not code. The batch 167 timing drives TensorFlow.js 4.22.0 and
tfjs-backend-webgpu 4.22.0 as a black box from the WebGPU backend README
(https://github.com/tensorflow/tfjs/blob/master/tfjs-backend-webgpu/README.md) and the API pages for `matMul`,
`spectral.fft`, `sum`, `complex`, `real` and `imag` (https://js.tensorflow.org/api/latest/). The library source was
not opened. NumPy 2.5.4, outside the repository, is the correctness oracle for those three sizes.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| WGSL reflection, host-shareable layout, f16 | W3C WebGPU Shading Language (resource interface; alignment and size table); W3C WebGPU (bind group layouts, timestamp queries, indirect dispatch); IEEE 754-2019 binary16 |
| Broadcasting, type promotion, `take`, `histogram`, `quantile` (linear), `argsort`, `mod`/`floorDiv`, `unique`, `bincount`, `convolve` modes | NumPy reference documentation (broadcasting rules; NEP 50 "Promotion rules for Python scalars") |
| `convolve2d` modes with zero fill | SciPy `scipy.signal.convolve2d` documentation |
| Reduce tree, scan | W. D. Hillis and G. L. Steele, "Data parallel algorithms", CACM 29(12), 1986; G. E. Blelloch, "Prefix sums and their applications", CMU-CS-90-190, 1990 |
| Segmented scan operator | J. T. Schwartz, "Ultracomputers", ACM TOPLAS 2(4), 1980; Blelloch 1990, section 1.5 |
| Radix sort | N. Satish, M. Harris and M. Garland, "Designing efficient sorting algorithms for manycore GPUs", IPDPS 2009 |
| Matrix product tiling | V. Volkov and J. W. Demmel, "Benchmarking GPUs to tune dense linear algebra", SC08 |
| FFT, Stockham radix-2 | N. K. Govindaraju, B. Lloyd, Y. Dotsenko, B. Smith and J. Manferdelli, "High performance discrete Fourier transforms on graphics processors", SC08; C. Van Loan, "Computational Frameworks for the Fast Fourier Transform", SIAM 1992 |
| FFT of any length | L. I. Bluestein, "A linear filtering approach to the computation of the discrete Fourier transform", IEEE Trans. Audio Electroacoustics 18(4), 1970 |
| Random numbers | J. K. Salmon, M. A. Moraes, R. O. Dror and D. E. Shaw, "Parallel random numbers: as easy as 1, 2, 3", SC11 (Philox4x32-10 constants and the published known-answer vectors, used as test data); G. E. P. Box and M. E. Muller, Ann. Math. Statist. 29(2), 1958; D. Lemire, "Fast random integer generation in an interval", ACM TOMACS 29(1), 2019 |
| `log1p`, `expm1` | D. Goldberg, "What every computer scientist should know about floating-point arithmetic", ACM Computing Surveys 23(1), 1991 |
| Streaming mean and variance | T. F. Chan, G. H. Golub and R. J. LeVeque, "Algorithms for computing the sample variance", Amer. Statistician 37(3), 1983 |
| Sparse CSR, SpMV, `coo`, `csr`, `diags` | SciPy `scipy.sparse` documentation (formats and constructor semantics); N. Bell and M. Garland, "Implementing sparse matrix-vector multiplication on throughput-oriented processors", SC09 |
| Conjugate gradient, BiCGSTAB, Jacobi preconditioning | M. R. Hestenes and E. Stiefel, J. Res. NBS 49(6), 1952; H. A. van der Vorst, SIAM J. Sci. Stat. Comput. 13(2), 1992; Y. Saad, "Iterative Methods for Sparse Linear Systems", 2nd ed., SIAM 2003, algorithms 9.1 and 7.7 |
| Reverse-mode differentiation | A. Griewank and A. Walther, "Evaluating Derivatives: Principles and Techniques of Algorithmic Differentiation", 2nd ed., SIAM 2008, chapter 3; derivative rules from standard calculus tables |
| CPU fallback (interpreter, LSD radix sort, host kernels) | The WGSL conversion and arithmetic rules above; D. E. Knuth, "The Art of Computer Programming" vol. 3, section 5.2.5 (radix sorting); the package's own WGSL kernels, transcribed |

## Data track: `@margoengine/sciviz`

Written clean room from the sources below. No source code of plotting or visualisation libraries (plotly, vtk.js,
deck.gl, regl, three.js, matplotlib, ParaView, napari, ipyvolume, anywidget and the like) was read. Margo's own colour maps are
computed from their published definitions. The five standard maps are the authors' published tables, recorded as data
under the standard-file exception documented above (THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md, `packages/sciviz/NOTICE.md`). The batch 167
timing drives VTK.js 38.0.5 as a black box from the Volume, ImageData, DataArray, ColorTransferFunction,
PiecewiseFunction, VolumeMapper, Camera and FullScreenRenderWindow pages at https://kitware.github.io/vtk-js/api/.
The library source was not opened.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Volume ray marching | J. Kruger and R. Westermann, "Acceleration techniques for GPU-based volume rendering", IEEE Visualization 2003 (front-to-back compositing, early ray termination); opacity correction for the step length from the emission-absorption model (N. Max, "Optical models for direct volume rendering", IEEE TVCG 1(2), 1995) |
| Marching cubes table (derived, not copied) | W. E. Lorensen and H. E. Cline, "Marching cubes", SIGGRAPH 1987; face-consistent disambiguation as required by G. M. Nielson and B. Hamann, "The asymptotic decider", IEEE Visualization 1991 |
| Tick spacing | P. S. Heckbert, "Nice numbers for graph labels", Graphics Gems, 1990 |
| Streamlines and quiver glyphs | Classical fourth-order Runge-Kutta (W. Kutta, 1901), as in E. Hairer, S. P. Norsett and G. Wanner, *Solving Ordinary Differential Equations I*, section II.1; trilinear interpolation of the gridded field |
| Series colours | M. Okabe and K. Ito, "Color Universal Design (CUD): how to make figures and presentations that are friendly to colorblind people", 2008 (the published palette values) |
| Date ticks | Calendar arithmetic in UTC from ECMAScript's `Date.UTC` (ECMA-262, section 21.4); log ticks at decades (Heckbert 1990's nice numbers in log space) |
| Histogram bins | D. Freedman and P. Diaconis, "On the histogram as a density estimator: L2 theory", Z. Wahrscheinlichkeitstheorie verw. Gebiete 57, 1981; H. A. Sturges, "The choice of a class interval", JASA 21(153), 1926 |
| Lasso selection (point in polygon) | M. Shimrat, "Algorithm 112: Position of point relative to polygon", CACM 5(8), 1962 (even-odd crossing rule) |
| PNG and SVG export | W3C SVG 1.1 (Second Edition) recommendation; the WHATWG HTML standard's canvas 2D context and OffscreenCanvas `convertToBlob` |
| Surface picking | Ray-height-field intersection by stepping and bisection on the bilinear surface (standard numerical root bracketing; W. H. Press et al., *Numerical Recipes*, 3rd ed., section 9.1) |
| cubehelix | D. A. Green, Bull. Astr. Soc. India 39, 289 (2011) |
| coolwarm | K. Moreland, "Diverging color maps for scientific visualization", ISVC 2009 |
| viridis, magma, inferno, plasma (recorded data, CC0) | N. Smith and S. van der Walt, mpl-colormaps, https://github.com/BIDS/colormap (the authors' table file `colormaps.py`; data only) |
| cividis (recorded data, CC BY 4.0) | J. R. Nuñez, C. R. Anderton and R. S. Renslow, PLOS ONE 13(7): e0199239 (2018), supporting file S2 |
| OKLab and the perceptual maps | B. Ottosson, "A perceptual color space for image processing" (2020); sRGB transfer from IEC 61966-2-1; CIELAB and D65 from CIE 15:2004 |
| NRRD volumes in a figure (`sciviz/nrrd`) | Teem project, "Definition of NRRD File Format", https://teem.sourceforge.net/nrrd/format.html, read in full (2026-11-07), and its general description descformat.html; the reading itself is `loaders/nrrd`, row LF5 |
| NIfTI-1 and NIfTI-2 headers (`sciviz/formats`) | NIfTI Data Format Working Group: the annotated header definitions nifti1.h (1528 lines) and nifti2.h, read in full (2026-11-07) from the NIFTI-Imaging nifti_clib copies because the NIMH copies were offline (header files only, no library code), and the NIfTI-2 committee document nifti2_doc.html on NITRC (signature bytes, version and byte-swap test), read in full |
| DICOM files and series (`sciviz/formats`) | NEMA DICOM edition 2026d, read in full (2026-11-07): PS3.10 chapter 7 (preamble, DICM prefix, File Meta Information); PS3.5 6.2 and 6.2.2 (VRs, UN), 6.4, 7.1 to 7.6 (element encoding, undefined lengths, items and delimiters), chapter 8 (8.1, 8.2 intro and 8.2.1, native pixel data), chapter 10, annex A.1, A.2, A.5 (deflate) and the retired A.3 (explicit VR big endian, 2016b edition); PS3.6 dictionary rows of every element read; PS3.3 10.7.1.3, C.7.6.2 with C.7.6.2.1.1 (Image Plane), C.7.6.3 with C.7.6.3.1.2 to C.7.6.3.1.4 (Image Pixel), C.7.6.6 (Multi-frame) and C.11.1 (Modality LUT and rescale) |
| Zarr arrays (`sciviz/formats`) | Read in full (2026-11-07) at https://zarr-specs.readthedocs.io: Zarr storage specification version 2, Zarr core specification 3.1 (the 3.0 URL redirects to it; 3.1 adds short-hand names and the `must_understand` rule) with its regular chunk grid, chunk key encodings, data types and fill values, and the bytes, gzip, crc32c and transpose codec pages; CRC-32C from RFC 3720 section 12.1 and appendix B.4 (polynomial 0x1EDC6F41, reflected, test vectors), read in full, which cites J. Castagnoli, S. Brauer and M. Herrmann, IEEE Trans. Communications 41(6), 1993 (not read: the RFC gives everything the code uses); gzip, zlib and DEFLATE from RFC 1952, 1950 and 1951 through `packages/shared/inflate.js` |
| Unstructured grids and legacy VTK files (`sciviz/grids`) | Kitware, "VTK File Formats" (from The VTK User's Guide, VTK 4.2 edition PDF, all 19 pages) and the current "Simple Legacy Formats" page of the VTK documentation, read in full: the five file parts, ASCII and big-endian BINARY data, UNSTRUCTURED_GRID with CELLS and CELL_TYPES, the attribute sections, and the point order of each linear cell in figure 2 (with the TestLinearCellDemo picture); the VTK 9.7 release note "VTK Wedge Cell Types: Fix Point Ordering, Triangulation and Volume Correctness" for the corrected wedge order. Surface and slice algorithms written from the cell definitions. |
| Force-directed graph layout (`sciviz/graph`) | T. M. J. Fruchterman and E. M. Reingold, "Graph Drawing by Force-directed Placement", Software: Practice and Experience 21(11), 1129-1164, 1991, read in full: the two forces, k from the area per vertex, the temperature limit, quench and simmer cooling, coincident vertices, the frame and the grid variant. J. Barnes and P. Hut, "A hierarchical O(N log N) force-calculation algorithm", Nature 324, 446-449, 1986: only the abstract is free to read; the method was taken from the same authors' "Error analysis of a tree code", ApJS 70, 389-417, 1989 (NASA ADS scan), section I and appendix A read in full: insertion into cubic cells, aggregate mass and centre of mass, the opening test side < theta d, and the note that a cell holding the particle can pass it. J. Dubinski, "A parallel tree code", New Astronomy 1, 133-147, 1996 (arXiv astro-ph/9603097), section 2.2 read in full: Barnes's 1994 opening test d > l / theta + delta, delta the offset of the centre of mass from the cell's geometric centre, and Salmon and Warren's 1994 finding (J. Comp. Phys. 111, 136, not free to read) that the plain test errs grossly when the mass sits near the cell's edge. |
| Map projections and slippy-map tiles (`sciviz/geo`) | J. P. Snyder, "Map Projections: A Working Manual", USGS Professional Paper 1395, 1987 (USGS scan), read in full for the parts used: chapter 7 (Mercator, the sphere forms 7-1, 7-2, 7-4 and the note on adding or taking 360 degrees from the longitude difference), table 7 (sphere northings checked by the tests), chapter 12 (equidistant cylindrical, 12-1 to 12-3), table 1 and equation 4-20 for the radius of curvature in the prime vertical N. WGS 84 constants a = 6378137 m and 1/f = 298.257223563 from NIMA TR8350.2, 3rd edition. Tile numbering (2^z by 2^z squares of the Web Mercator world, x from 180 W, y from the north) derived here from the Mercator formulas; no tile code read. |

## Voxel files: `@margoengine/voxel-edit/formats`

VF1 to VF6: file layouts written from each format's public description; no reader or writer of another project was read.

VF7 to VF12 (`.gox` and `.vxm`, 2026-11-08) have no public specification, so they were written in a two-person clean
room that Scott approved on 2026-11-08. A spec writer read Goxel (GPL-3.0) `src/formats/gox.c` and the files it calls at
commit 33be5c8, vengi (MIT) `GoxFormat` and `VXMFormat` at commit 3471178, and one blog post that restates Goxel's header
comment, decoded eight vengi test files (CC BY-SA 3.0), and wrote a prose specification with byte tables and no code.
The implementer read only that specification: no Goxel, vengi or VoxEdit code, and no web search for code. The sample
files were used in a local check only and are not in this repository.

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| VF1 | Qubicle `.qb`: six u32 header fields, matrix name and size, z y x cell order, CODEFLAG 2 and NEXTSLICEFLAG 6 RLE | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Minddesk, "Qubicle Binary" file format description (Qubicle documentation) |
| VF2 | Qubicle Exchange `.qef`: text header, 0 to 1 colours, one `x y z index mask` line per voxel | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Minddesk, "Qubicle Exchange Format" description (Qubicle documentation) |
| VF3 | `binvox`: text header, then value and count byte pairs; x slowest, then z, y fastest | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Patrick Min, "binvox voxel file format specification" |
| VF4 | KVX: header, column offsets, slab records of top, length, cull and colours; 6 bit palette in the last 768 bytes | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Ken Silverman, the KVX description in the SLAB6 documentation |
| VF5 | VXL: columns of spans N, S, E, A with BGRA dwords for top and bottom colours; N of 0 ends a column | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Ken Silverman, the VXL format description in the Voxlap documentation |
| VF6 | over 255 colours: drop the same low bits of each channel until 255 remain, keeping the bucket's centre | voxel-edit/src/formats.ts | Margo-own: deterministic, plain bit truncation; median cut is in the `./voxelize` entry |
| VF7 | Goxel `.gox`: `GOX ` and version 2 (1 shifts block corners by 8), chunks of type, i32 length, data and a CRC every writer leaves 0; dictionaries of i32 key and value lengths | voxel-edit/src/gox.ts | Clean-room specification FORMATS-SPEC.md section 1 (two-person process, below) |
| VF8 | `.gox` BL16 block: a 64 x 64 RGBA PNG, voxel (x, y, z) at byte 4(x + 16y + 256z), alpha 0 air; LAYR records (index, x, y, z, 0) place blocks at any corner, later over earlier | voxel-edit/src/gox.ts | FORMATS-SPEC.md 1.8.3 and 1.8.4; PNG from the W3C PNG specification through `@margoengine/decoders/png` and `packages/shared/png.js` |
| VF9 | `.gox` shape layers (sphere, cube, cylinder in the unit-cube box `mat`, voxel centres inside) and clone layers (`base_id`, voxels moved by `mat`); file Z up turned to Y up as cell (x, z, -y) | voxel-edit/src/gox.ts | FORMATS-SPEC.md 1.7, 1.8.4 and 1.9; the cell mapping and edge rule are Margo-own |
| VF10 | VoxEdit `.vxm`: `VXM` and a version character 3 to 9, A to D; the per-version field sequence (size, pivot, header surface, LOD header, mesh records, full palettes, materials, models, trailer) | voxel-edit/src/vxm.ts | FORMATS-SPEC.md 2.3 to 2.6, 2.10 and 2.11 |
| VF11 | `.vxm` materials B, G, R, A plus an emissive byte; full palettes R, G, B, A; index 255 air; (length, index) runs filling cells Z fastest, then Y, then X, file X mirrored | voxel-edit/src/vxm.ts | FORMATS-SPEC.md 2.7 to 2.9 |
| VF12 | `.vxm` version 12 writing: no baked mesh, one empty LOD, magenta unused palette entries, one trailer byte 0 | voxel-edit/src/vxm.ts | FORMATS-SPEC.md 2.12 |

## Smooth voxel meshes: `@margoengine/voxel-edit/smooth`

Written from the paper below; no Surface Nets code of another project was read.

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SN1 | one point per net cell the surface crosses, at the mean of its edge crossings; one quad per filled-to-air sample pair; relaxation toward neighbours kept inside the cell | voxel-edit/src/smooth.ts | Sarah Gibson, "Constrained Elastic Surface Nets", MERL TR-99-24 / MICCAI 1998 |
| SN2 | normals from the density difference across a net cell; a separable box blur by running sums | voxel-edit/src/smooth.ts | Margo-own, central differences and the standard running sum box filter |

## Mesh to voxels: `@margoengine/voxel-edit/voxelize`

Written from the papers below; no voxelizer of another project was read.

| id | formula | file | source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| VZ1 | triangle against box by separating axes: three box normals, the triangle normal, nine edge crosses | voxel-edit/src/voxelize.ts | Tomas Akenine-Moller, "Fast 3D Triangle-Box Overlap Testing", Journal of Graphics Tools, 2001 |
| VZ2 | solid by crossing parity along x rows through cell centres, the hit point from barycentric coordinates | voxel-edit/src/voxelize.ts | Moller and Trumbore, "Fast, Minimum Storage Ray/Triangle Intersection", Journal of Graphics Tools, 1997 |
| VZ3 | shared edges on a ray: each edge read in one fixed vertex order and owning its low end, so a closed mesh crosses evenly | voxel-edit/src/voxelize.ts | Margo-own, the half-open crossing rule for point in polygon |
| VZ4 | palette: cut the box of widest channel range at its weighted median, then box means | voxel-edit/src/voxelize.ts | Paul Heckbert, "Color Image Quantization for Frame Buffer Display", SIGGRAPH 1982 |

## Rigging: `@margoengine/rigging`

Written clean room from the papers and specifications below. No source code of rigging tools or engines (Blender,
Maya, Pinocchio, libigl, three.js, Babylon, Godot, Unity, Unreal and the like) was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Bone heat weights | I. Baran and J. Popović, "Automatic rigging and animation of 3D characters", ACM SIGGRAPH 2007 (the equilibrium (−Δ + H) w = H p, heat c / d² from the nearest visible bone, shared heat at equidistant bones) |
| Cotangent Laplacian and vertex areas | U. Pinkall and K. Polthier, "Computing discrete minimal surfaces and their conjugates", Experimental Mathematics 2(1), 1993; M. Meyer, M. Desbrun, P. Schröder and A. H. Barr, "Discrete differential-geometry operators for triangulated 2-manifolds", VisMath 2002 (published 2003) |
| Conjugate gradients, Jacobi preconditioning | M. R. Hestenes and E. Stiefel, J. Res. NBS 49(6), 1952; Y. Saad, "Iterative Methods for Sparse Linear Systems", 2nd ed., SIAM 2003, algorithm 9.1 |
| Skin data rules (checks) | The Khronos glTF 2.0 specification, section 3.7.3.3 (skinned mesh attributes: JOINTS_n / WEIGHTS_n, weights summing to 1, joint indices within the skin) |
| Brush falloff | The smoothstep polynomial 3t² − 2t³ (Hermite interpolation, standard numerical analysis) |
| IK chains (`rigging/ik`) | A. Aristidou and J. Lasenby, "FABRIK: a fast, iterative solver for the inverse kinematics problem", Graphical Models 73(5), 2011 (backward and forward reaching passes); the pole as a rotation of each inner joint about the line through its neighbours, which keeps both bone lengths (elementary geometry) |
| Elbow evaluator | The law of cosines for the two-bone triangle and Rodrigues' rotation formula (O. Rodrigues, 1840) about the shoulder-target line |
| Shortest-arc rotations, quaternion from a matrix | Quaternion algebra from W. R. Hamilton's definition; S. W. Shepperd, "Quaternion from rotation matrix", J. Guidance and Control 1(3), 1978 (through `@margoengine/math/quat`) |
| Root motion (`animator`) | Integration of a sampled track over an interval split into whole loop cycles and partial ones (elementary); `smoothing`, a centred moving average of that track, carried on by whole laps for a loop and by point reflection through the ends of a clip that stops (elementary); the travel carried through the node hierarchy's local rotations and scales (glTF 2.0 section 3.5.3, transformations) |
| Look-at and aim (`animator`) | Shortest-arc turns of the look axis toward the target shared along a bone chain, a roll about the look axis taking the up axis's projection to world up's (vector rejection), and slerp (K. Shoemake, "Animating rotation with quaternion curves", SIGGRAPH 1985) for weights |
| Retarget presets (`rigging/retarget`) | Bone names from public documentation only: the VRM 1.0 humanoid bone list (VRM Consortium specification, `VRMC_vrm` humanoid), Godot's documented `SkeletonProfileHumanoid` bone names, Roblox's documented R15 body parts, and the bone names visible in Mixamo-exported files; rest-pose alignment is a shortest-arc turn per bone (quaternion algebra above), parents first |
| Spring bones (`rigging/springs`) | The VRMC_springBone 1.0 specification (VRM Consortium), its fields and its non-normative algorithm section; L. Verlet, "Computer experiments on classical fluids", Physical Review 159, 1967; the time-corrected step and the damped spring are elementary mechanics |
| VRM avatars (`rigging/vrm`) | The VRMC_vrm 1.0 and VRMC_springBone 1.0 specifications (VRM Consortium) and the glTF 2.0 binary container specification (Khronos); the two sample avatars are data, read and never studied for code |
| Voxel weights (`rigging/voxel-weights`) | O. Dionne and M. de Lasa, "Geodesic voxel binding for production character meshes", SCA 2013; the fill vote for open meshes is ours |
| Automatic rig (`rigging/autorig`) | I. Baran and J. Popović, "Automatic rigging and animation of 3D characters", ACM SIGGRAPH 2007 (the idea of finding a skeleton from the solid inside a mesh; their embedding is not used, the placement by paths and cuts is ours); D. Winter, "Biomechanics and Motor Control of Human Movement" (body segment lengths); voxel grid as in the row above |
| Corrective shapes (`rigging/correctives`) | J. P. Lewis, M. Cordner and N. Fong, "Pose space deformation", SIGGRAPH 2000, for the radial basis interpolation; the single-joint reading is plain rotation-vector projection |
| Weight transfer (`rigging/transfer`) | R. Abdrashitov, K. Raichstat, J. Monsen and D. Hill, "Robust skin weights transfer via weight inpainting", SIGGRAPH Asia 2023 Technical Communications, for matching by distance and normal; the fill is a plain weighted average, not its inpainting solve |
| Face tracking (`rigging/face`) | Apple's published `ARFaceAnchor.BlendShapeLocation` names, Google's MediaPipe Face Landmarker output format and the VRM 1.0 expression presets; no tracker's code was read |

## Game AI: `@margoengine/behavior`

Written clean room from the papers, talks and textbook descriptions below. No source code of behaviour-tree, utility-AI
or steering libraries or engines (Unreal, Unity, Godot, BehaviorTree.CPP, OpenSteer, three.js, Babylon, Yuka and the
like) was read. The batch 167 timing drives Yuka 0.7.8 as a black box from its documented flocking example
(https://github.com/Mugen87/yuka/blob/master/examples/steering/flocking/index.html): one shared alignment, cohesion and
separation behaviour, and no wander. The library source was not opened.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Behaviour trees (sequence, selector, parallel policies, decorators, reactive re-checks, halting) | M. Colledanchise and P. Ögren, "Behavior Trees in Robotics and AI: An Introduction", CRC Press 2018 (arXiv:1709.00084), chapters 1-3; D. Isla, "Handling complexity in the Halo 2 AI", GDC 2005 |
| Utility scoring, response curves, compensation factor | D. Mark, "Behavioral Mathematics for Game AI", Course Technology 2009; D. Mark and K. Dill, "Improving AI decision modeling through utility theory", GDC AI Summit 2010; "Building a better centaur: AI at massive scale", GDC 2015 (`score + (1 - score)(1 - 1/n) score`) |
| Steering (seek, flee, arrive, pursue, evade, wander, obstacle avoidance, path following, priorities) | C. W. Reynolds, "Steering Behaviors For Autonomous Characters", Game Developers Conference 1999 |
| Flocking (separation, alignment, cohesion) | C. W. Reynolds, "Flocks, herds and schools: a distributed behavioral model", ACM SIGGRAPH 1987 |
| Seeded random stream | `@margoengine/math`'s `createRandom` (mulberry32) |
| Perception (`behavior/perception`) | M. Walsh, "Modeling Perception and Awareness in Tom Clancy's Splinter Cell Blacklist", Game AI Pro 2, chapter 28, 2015 (read in full): perceiving against being aware, test points on the body, vision shapes with a timer scaled by distance, coffin-shaped boxes past a cone, sounds with a radius and a priority, sound distance that is not straight. Its room graph (TEAS) is left to `soundDistance`. Where its text says the "most inclusive" shape sets the time, Margo takes the shortest of the shapes holding the target. D. Isla, B. Blumberg, "Object Persistence for Synthetic Creatures", AAMAS 2002 (read in full): the Probabilistic Occupancy Map, equations 1.3 to 1.11. Equation 1.4 is hard to read in the copy read, and Margo reads it as a constant rate plus max(0, v.l / l.l); shares here are per neighbour, so the total holds at walls, and the grid is square where the paper's is hexagonal. |
| Environment queries (`behavior/eqs`) | M. Jack, "Tactical Position Selection: An Architecture and Query Language", Game AI Pro, chapter 26, 2013 (read in full): generation, conditions, weights, query options as fallbacks, weights normalized to 0..1, the directness measure of equation 26.1, cheap criteria first and the binary heap of 26.7.4 on the highest potential score. The chapter keeps a lowest and a highest score and can skip a weight once a point has won; Margo keeps the highest only and evaluates the winner fully, so `score` is exact. Criteria are functions here, not the chapter's text language, and the timeslicing of 26.7.5 is left out. |
| Goal planner (`behavior/goap`) | J. Orkin, "Symbolic Representation of Game World State: Toward Real-Time Planning in Games", AAAI Workshop on Challenges in Game AI, 2004 (read in full): symbols of the world, preconditions and effects, goals and actions that never name each other, the regressive A* search, and context preconditions, here `available`. J. Orkin, "Agent Architecture Considerations for Real-Time Planning in Games", AIIDE 2005 (read in full): action costs, planning again when a plan breaks, a failure recording why. J. Orkin, "Three States and a Plan: The A.I. of F.E.A.R.", GDC 2006 (only its differences from STRIPS read). R. Fikes, N. Nilsson, "STRIPS: A New Approach to the Application of Theorem Proving to Problem Solving", SRI Technical Note 43, 1970 (read in full; the 1971 journal paper was not read): operators with preconditions and effects, and unnamed facts staying as they were. Orkin 2003 was not read, since no author copy was found. Fixed-value symbols replace the first-order clauses of STRIPS, and a symbol that holds now stays needed, because an earlier step may undo it. |
| Smart objects (`behavior/smart-objects`) | M. Kallmann, D. Thalmann, "Modeling Objects for Interaction Tasks", Eurographics Workshop on Animation and Simulation, 1998 (read in full): objects that hold their own interaction positions, state variables and behaviours, behaviours available or not by that state, a door serving several agents with spare positions. Its script language and part commands are left to `run`. K. Forbus, W. Wright, "Some notes on programming objects in The Sims", 2001 (read up to section 4.1; the rest is a worked example): objects advertising what they offer against an agent's needs, faded by distance, with a test that hides an interaction. The scoring formula here is Margo's own: the note gives the idea, not this sum. Slots with claims and `exclusive` are Margo's additions. |
| Flow fields (`behavior/flowfield`) | E. Emerson, "Crowd Pathfinding and Steering Using Flow Field Tiles", Game AI Pro, chapter 23, 2013 (read in full): the cost, integration and flow fields, 255 as a wall, line of sight flags near the goal ended by any cost above one, eight-way flow, cost stamps that keep the old costs, sliding along walls, island tests. Its sectors, portals, tile cache and merging A* are left out. The chapter marks sight with corners and Bresenham lines; Margo grows rings from the goal instead, and integrates every cell, those in sight too. A. Treuille, S. Cooper, Z. Popovic, "Continuum Crowds", SIGGRAPH 2006 (section 4.3 only): fast marching with KNOWN and CANDIDATE cells on a heap, and the larger root of the upwind finite difference, equation 11. J. Tsitsiklis 1995, which that section refers to, was not read. |
| Task network planner (`behavior/htn`) | T. Humphreys, "Exploring HTN Planners through Example", Game AI Pro, chapter 12, 2013 (read in full): world state, primitive and compound tasks, methods in priority order, the forward decomposition loop with its decomposition history, plan checking at run time, recursion, expected effects, the method traversal record, not planning on a task's own effects, partial plans written into the domain. The chapter accepts a new plan when all its method indexes are equal or higher; Margo compares records at the first index that differs, since those after it belong to different branches. Its second use of the record, culling during the search, and two planners for two body parts are left out. D. Nau et al. on SHOP (1999) and K. Erol, J. Hendler, D. Nau on HTN semantics were downloaded and not read, and nothing here comes from them. |
| Context steering (`behavior/context`) | A. Fray, "Context Steering: Behavior-Driven Steering at the Macro Scale", Game AI Pro 2, chapter 18, 2015 (read in full): interest and danger maps as rings of slots, merging by the highest value, the mask from the lowest danger, the strongest interest left, subslot movement from the gradients, a spread that falls off, blur, and a share of the last map for stability. `slack` and `steer` are Margo's additions, not the chapter's, and its racing lane mode (18.5) is left out. |
| XR interaction toolkit (`xr/interaction`) | WebXR Device API (targetRaySpace along -Z, gripSpace), WebXR Gamepads Module (xr-standard trigger and squeeze), WebXR Hand Input Module (thumb and index tip joints); ray against plane and sphere from textbook geometry; the held pose is the grip pose times the offset taken at the grab |
| XR layers, haptics, video and locomotion (`xr/layers`) | WebXR Layers Module (composition layer types, layouts, isStatic and needsRedraw, the render state layers array, XRMediaBinding), except sections 6.1 to 6.3 and 6.9 (the WebGL binding); WebXR/WebGPU Binding draft (layer creation, getSubImage, array layers per eye, cube face order); WebXR Device API getOffsetReferenceSpace and XRRigidTransform; Gamepad (vibrationActuator, dual-rumble) and Gamepad Extensions (hapticActuators, pulse); the teleport arc is projectile motion under constant gravity |
| XR planes, depth and light (`xr/ar`) | WebXR Plane Detection Module (XRPlane, XRPlaneSet, the update planes algorithm), WebXR Depth Sensing Module (feature, session configuration, XRSession attributes, XRDepthInformation, XRCPUDepthInformation and getDepthInformation; the GPU depth sections skimmed only) and WebXR Lighting Estimation API (XRLightProbe, XRLightEstimate, requestLightProbe, getLightEstimate; the reflection cube map sections skimmed only); WebXR Anchors Module read in full (anchors stay with `HitTest`); ear clipping after Meisters, "Polygons have ears" (1975), from its description, the paper itself not read |
| XR controller and hand models (`xr/models`) | WebXR Device API section 10.1 XRInputSource (gripSpace axes, skipRendering); WebXR Gamepads Module section 3 (the xr-standard mapping and placeholders); WebXR Hand Input Module sections 3 and 4 (joint order, joint orientation, radius, whole-hand tracking); WebXR Input Profiles registry and assets READMEs (profilesList, layouts, visual responses), read in part. The rest of the Device API was not reread for this entry. |
| XR through the engine (`xr` `createXREngine`, `EyeViews`) | WebXR/WebGPU binding draft (xrCompatible adapters, getViewSubImage, getViewDescriptor, projection depth range [0, 1] with depthNear at 0), read in full; WebXR Device API XRView and XRRigidTransform sections |
| XR device runs (`xr/device-check`) | W3C WebXR Device API (XRSession.enabledFeatures, XRViewerPose.views, XRPose.emulatedPosition, XRInputSource) and the WebXR Hand Input Module (XRInputSource.hand), read for those interfaces; the WebXR/WebGPU binding draft, read in full |

## Snapshot playback: `@margoengine/net`

Written clean room from the papers and specifications below. No source of a networking library, engine or audio tool was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Adaptive render delay (`net/delay`): the receiver sets its delay from what it measured, no synchronised clocks, loss against delay as the trade | **Read in full:** R. Ramjee, J. Kurose, D. Towsley, H. Schulzrinne, "Adaptive Playout Mechanisms for Packetized Audio Applications in Wide-Area Networks", IEEE INFOCOM 1994 |
| The delay as a percentile of the last arrivals, a spike followed and not learned from, its `head` 4 and `tail` 2, the 99% point | **Read in full:** S. B. Moon, J. Kurose, D. Towsley, "Packet audio playout delay adjustment: performance bounds and algorithms", Multimedia Systems 6, 1998, its Algorithm 3. Margo's own: measuring the gap to the newest snapshot held, which folds the send interval and loss into one number; a window of 256 where the paper keeps 10,000 packets; a clock that slews where audio moves in silence; a long spike counting as a new route, for the weakness the paper names in section 4.3 |
| Any field (`net/fields`): colour blended in linear light | The sRGB transfer function of IEC 61966-2-1, through `@margoengine/math/color`, already recorded there. The field list, loop seams and clip changes are Margo's own |
| Shortest-arc slerp, cubic Hermite position | K. Shoemake, "Animating Rotation with Quaternion Curves", SIGGRAPH 1985, and the textbook Hermite basis. Both predate this work in `SnapshotBuffer`; neither was re-read for it |
| Mean deviation as the measure of jitter (considered; a percentile serves better here, by Moon's results) | **Read:** RFC 3550 section 6.4.1 and RFC 6298 section 2 only |

## Game networking: `@margoengine/netcode`

Written clean room from the articles, talks and specifications below. No source code of networking libraries or
engines (Colyseus, Photon, Mirror, GGPO, Rapier, Unity Netcode, Unreal and the like) was read. The batch 167 timing
publishes rollback resimulation on Margo's own loopback. GGPO is C++ and Colyseus is not a rollback library, so no
JavaScript package was timed against it.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Fixed timestep loop | G. Fiedler, "Fix Your Timestep!" (2004) |
| Snapshots, deltas against an acknowledged baseline, quantization | G. Fiedler, "Snapshot Compression" and "State Synchronization" (2015); the delta-against-acknowledged-snapshot idea as described in public talks on the Quake 3 network model |
| Client prediction, reconciliation, entity interpolation | G. Gambetta, "Fast-Paced Multiplayer" parts I to IV; Y. Bernier, "Latency Compensating Methods in Client/Server In-game Protocol Design and Optimization", GDC 2001 |
| Client running ahead by the one-way delay, input redundancy | T. Ford, "Overwatch Gameplay Architecture and Netcode", GDC 2017 |
| Clock synchronisation | F. Cristian, "Probabilistic clock synchronization", Distributed Computing 3(3), 1989 (the smallest round trip bounds the offset) |
| Rollback (input delay, prediction as the last input, save, load and resimulate, frame-advantage time sync, checksums) | T. Cannon, "Fight the Lag! The Trick Behind GGPO's Low-Latency Netcode", Game Developer magazine, September 2012; M. Stallone, "8 Frames in 16ms: Rollback Networking in Mortal Kombat and Injustice 2", GDC 2018 |
| Lag compensation (server-side rewind to the shooter's view) | Y. Bernier, GDC 2001 (above) |
| Interest management (`./interest`): the circle around the player, grid only as an index, `always` as class-based and `visible` as visibility-based interest | **Read in part (sections 2.2, 3.1 to 3.3, 5.2 and 6):** J.-S. Boulanger, J. Kienzle, C. Verbrugge, "Comparing Interest Management Algorithms for Massively Multiplayer Games", NetGames 2006, from text pulled out of the PDF, figures not seen. The leave radius and the grid's use as an exact index are Margo's |
| Priority accumulator, skip what does not fit and try the next, budget from the packet's bytes (`./interest`) | **Read in part:** G. Fiedler, "State Synchronization" (2015), its priority accumulator section. The repeat of unconfirmed values, frozen while anything waits, is Margo's answer to deltas against an acknowledged baseline. M. Frohnmayer, T. Gift, "The Tribes Engine Networking Model", GDC 2000: **not read**, no copy from its authors found |
| Hash of grid cells (`./interest`) | M. Teschner et al., "Optimized Spatial Hashing for Collision Detection of Deformable Objects", VMV 2003: the three primes only, as `@margoengine/water` records |
| Correction smoothing (`./smoothing`): the error offset that shrinks over time, a correction folded in so the drawn value does not move, a tighter factor for big errors | **Read in part:** G. Fiedler, "State Synchronization" (2015), its "Visual Smoothing" section. Half lives by the clock instead of a factor per frame, one vector per struct and the walk over a schema are Margo's |
| WebTransport connection (`./webtransport`): datagrams for 'unreliable', a length-prefixed stream for 'reliable', the session options | **Read in part:** W3C WebTransport, Candidate Recommendation (first 100,000 of 285,173 characters, through a summarising fetch). The close capsule in the test server is from draft-ietf-webtrans-http3. The framing and the fallback of a big datagram to the stream are Margo's. The test server uses the outside tool aioquic through its Python API; it is not shipped and no aioquic code is in a package |
| Reconnection and held seats (`./rooms`): the seat timer, the one-use token, a new connection taking over from a dead one | No outside source: built from the rooms entry's own join flow. The name `reconnectionToken` is the plain name developers search for; no library's source was read. Random bits from the Web Crypto `getRandomValues` |
| Signalling (`./signal`): the room server, the order of offers, answers and candidates | **Read in part:** RFC 8838 Trickle ICE (sections 9, 11, 17, 18: each candidate exactly once and in order, over the using protocol) and RFC 8829 JSEP (sections 3.5.2 and 4.1.19, on trickling and addIceCandidate). The rooms, the joiner offering, the holding of early candidates and the rate limit are Margo's |
| Extrapolation (`./extrapolate`): moving on in a line from the last two snapshots, a cap, a blend to the truth | **Read most of:** S. Aggarwal, H. Banavar, A. Khandelwal, S. Mukherjee, S. Rangarajan, "Accuracy in dead-reckoning based distributed multi-player games" (NetGames 2004). The cap in ticks and blending through the schema's own interpolation are Margo's |
| Rollback relay and spectators (`./relay`) | No outside source: the framing, the frame log and the spectator built from `RollbackSession`'s own message format. Spectators fed confirmed inputs is a common idea; no paper or library was read for it |
| Rewound boxes and capsules (`./hitboxes`) | Same source as the rewind above (Bernier 2001). The ray tests are the ones already in `@margoengine/math`; blending rotations uses its `Quaternion.slerp`. Going into each shape's own frame is Margo's |
| Ray against circle and sphere | The quadratic of \|o + s d - c\|² = r² (elementary geometry) |
| WebSocket server | RFC 6455, "The WebSocket Protocol" (handshake 4.2, framing 5.2, masking 5.3, fragmentation 5.4, control frames 5.5, close codes 7.4) |
| WebRTC data channels | W3C "WebRTC: Real-Time Communication in Browsers" (RTCDataChannelInit: ordered, maxRetransmits, negotiated, id) |
| Room API shape (`./rooms`) | Public documentation of the room-and-matchmaking API shape common to web game servers (lifecycle hook names, joinOrCreate and friends, state patches at a patch rate); the wire format, matchmaking and state sync are this package's own |
| Code generator (`./generate`) | This package's own wire format (packages/netcode/README.md, "Wire format") |
| Varints, zigzag | LEB128 as specified in DWARF 4 section 7.6; zigzag as described in the Protocol Buffers encoding documentation |
| Schema hash | FNV-1a 32-bit as published by G. Fowler, L. C. Noll and K.-P. Vo |

## 3D Tiles: `@margoengine/tiles`

Written clean room from the specifications and papers below. No source code of 3D Tiles runtimes or tools (CesiumJS,
3d-tiles-renderer, three-loader-3dtiles, loaders.gl, 3d-tiles-tools, py3dtiles, iTwin.js and the like) was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Tileset JSON, refinement, geometric and screen-space error, bounding volumes, external tilesets, metadata | OGC 3D Tiles Specification 1.1 (OGC 22-025r4): "Tileset", "Tiles", "Bounding volumes", "Geometric error", "Refinement", "Metadata" |
| Implicit tiling, subtree files, Morton order | OGC 3D Tiles 1.1, "Implicit Tiling" (subtree binary format, availability bitstreams, template URIs, subdivision of boxes and regions) |
| b3dm, i3dm, pnts, cmpt, feature and batch tables | OGC 3D Tiles 1.1, "Tile formats" (the 1.0 formats 1.1 keeps) |
| y-up to z-up for glTF content | OGC 3D Tiles 1.1, "glTF transforms"; Khronos glTF 2.0, "Coordinate system and units" |
| WGS84, ECEF, east-north-up | NIMA TR8350.2 (WGS84 constants); B. R. Bowring, "Transformation from spatial to geographical coordinates", Survey Review 23 (181), 1976 |
| Octahedral normals (NORMAL_OCT16P, OCT32P) | Z. H. Cigolle, S. Donow, D. Evangelakos, M. Mara, M. McGuire and Q. Meyer, "A survey of efficient representations for independent unit vectors", JCGT 3(2), 2014 |
| Quaternion from a rotation matrix | S. W. Shepperd, "Quaternion from rotation matrix", J. Guidance and Control 1(3), 1978 |
| Oriented-box frustum and distance tests | Separating-axis projection of a box onto a plane normal (C. Ericson, "Real-Time Collision Detection", 2005, ch. 5) |
| Gaussian splat tiles (`tiles/splats`) | Khronos KHR_gaussian_splatting README (ratified); 3D Tiles 1.1 glTF content and `3DTILES_content_gltf`; the splats are read by gltf-pro and drawn by @margoengine/splats, both Margo's own |
| Quantized-mesh terrain (`tiles/terrain`) | CesiumGS/quantized-mesh README (quantized-mesh-1.0: header, zig-zag deltas, high-water-mark indices, edge lists, extensions 1, 2 and 4, layer.json, tms and EPSG:4326); OSGeo Tile Map Service specification; Web Mercator (EPSG:3857) as XYZ tile servers lay it out; skirts along tile edges are Margo's own way to hide level cracks |
| S2 cell volumes (`tiles/s2`) | CesiumGS/3d-tiles, `3DTILES_bounding_volume_S2` README (cell IDs, tokens, quadratic projection, extrusion, implicit subdivision and its availability figure); the S2 cell hierarchy guide, s2geometry.io/devguide/s2cell_hierarchy (faces, Hilbert curve, odd faces mirrored); D. Hilbert, "Ueber die stetige Abbildung einer Linie auf ein Flaechenstueck", Math. Annalen 38, 1891. No S2 library source was read |
| Hosted tileset sign-in and credits (`tiles/ion`) | Cesium ion REST API reference (GET /v1/assets/{id}/endpoint, bearer tokens, `attributions` with `collapsible`); Google Map Tiles API documentation for photorealistic 3D Tiles (`key` and `session` parameters, showing the visible tiles' copyright by frequency); Khronos glTF 2.0 `asset.copyright`; one refresh on HTTP 401 (RFC 6750) is Margo's own |

The batch 168 timing drives `3d-tiles-renderer` 0.4.21 as a black box from its README (https://github.com/NASA-AMMOS/3DTilesRendererJS): `TilesRenderer`, `setCamera`, `setResolution`, `errorTarget`, then `update`. The library source was not opened. `errorTarget` is set to 16 so both sides use one pixel budget (the README default is 6). The tileset is an explicit quadtree written for the measurement. Screen-space error follows OGC 3D Tiles 1.1, "Geometric error".

## Point clouds: LAS, octree and colours in `@margoengine/points`

Written clean room from the specifications and papers below. No source code of point-cloud libraries or viewers
(LAStools, LASzip, laz-perf, PDAL, libLAS, Potree, CloudCompare, Entwine and the like) was read.

The batch 168 timing drives `potree-core` 2.0.17 as a black box from its README (https://github.com/tentone/potree-core) and the types it publishes: `Potree`, `loadPointCloud`, `pointBudget`, `updatePointClouds`, `minNodePixelSize`, `material.size`, `PointSizeType.FIXED`. The library source was not opened. The folder is Potree 2.0 as that README describes a converter 2.1 output (metadata, hierarchy, one point file), written here in the DEFAULT layout our reader already tests. Both budgets are 10,000,000.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| LAS reader and writer | ASPRS LAS Specification 1.2 (2008), 1.3 R11 (2010) and 1.4 R15 (2019): public header block, VLR and EVLR headers, point data record formats 0 to 10, extra bytes descriptors (LASF_Spec record 4), OGC WKT (LASF_Projection 2112) |
| GeoTIFF keys of the CRS | OGC GeoTIFF Standard 1.1 (19-008r4): GeoKeyDirectoryTag layout, ProjectedCSTypeGeoKey 3072, GeographicTypeGeoKey 2048, VerticalCSTypeGeoKey 4096 |
| ASPRS classes | ASPRS LAS 1.4 R15, table 17 (standard point classes) |
| Nested-sample octree | Grid subsampling per node as described in M. Schütz, "Potree: Rendering Large Point Clouds in Web Browsers", diploma thesis, TU Wien, 2016 (the idea only); budget-ordered node selection by projected size |
| Seeded shuffle | Fisher-Yates (Knuth, TAOCP vol. 2, algorithm P) with the mulberry32 generator |
| Polygon area | Newell's method for a polygon's normal (M. E. Newell, 1972, as in F. S. Hill, "Computer Graphics", 1990) |
| LAZ decoder (`/laz`) and the test-only encoder | M. Isenburg, "LASzip: lossless compression of LiDAR data", Photogrammetric Engineering and Remote Sensing 79(2), 2013 (item predictors, return contexts, GPS time sequences, integer corrector coding); the LAZ Specification 1.4 R1 from rapidlasso (record "laszip encoded" 22204, compressors, item ids and versions, chunk table, layered point14 chunks, wave packets); I. H. Witten, R. M. Neal and J. G. Cleary, "Arithmetic coding for data compression", CACM 30(6), 1987, and A. Said, "Introduction to arithmetic coding: theory and practice", HP Labs HPL-2004-76, 2004 (32-bit range coder, adaptive bit and symbol models) |

Verified against a real USGS file: yes (2026-10-27). `packages/points/tests/fixtures/USGS_LPC_MT_Statewide_Phase3_2021_B21_568232.laz`
(public domain 3DEP tile, LAS 1.4, point format 6, written by LASzip 3.4.3, 54,634 points, two chunks) decodes to the point
count, the exact X/Y/Z minimum and maximum and the points-by-return array of the producer's header. It was also decoded
once by laspy 2.7.0 with lazrs 0.8.2 (installed in a throwaway virtual environment outside the repository and used only
through their public Python API as a black box; nothing from them is in the repository, package.json or the lock file).
Verified against laspy 2.7.0 / lazrs 0.8.2 on a real USGS file: all 54,634 points are field by field identical (raw
integer X, Y and Z, intensity, return number, number of returns, flag byte with scanner channel, classification, user
data, scan angle, point source id and the GPS time bit for bit; the 1,639,020 record bytes are equal). It exposed two errors that the
encoder-made files could not: in point14 the GPS time sequence model has 515 symbols (a new sequence is 511, the
switches 512 to 514), not the 516 of GPSTIME11 (LAZ specification 1.4, note 6 of the point14 item); and a multiplier 1
GPS point leaves the stored difference unchanged (the file is the authority; the specification text is silent). The
test encoder follows both readings.

Verified against real files in every point format: yes (2026-11-06). `packages/points/tests/laz-real.test.js` holds what
LASzip (through the `laszip` Python module) and lazrs 0.8.2 decode from public files of formats 0 to 3, 6, 7, 8 and 10,
and from real points LASzip itself compressed for formats 4, 5 and 9, as SHA-256 digests in
`packages/points/tests/fixtures/laz/records.json`. GPSTIME11, RGB12, RGB14, RGBNIR14, BYTE, BYTE14, WAVEPACKET13 and
WAVEPACKET14 are all covered, so the GPSTIME11 reading above is now checked on real files. Both decoders ran outside
the repository as black boxes through their public Python API; no source of LASzip, lazrs, laz-perf or laspy was read.
One rule came from that comparison and not from a text, because the specification is silent: the context passed to the
items after POINT14 is the new scanner channel only on a record where the channel changed, and 0 on every other record;
a record reads the last values of the record before it with the models of its own context, and a context first used in
a chunk takes a copy of those values. The test encoder follows this too. Item version 1, the coding of LASzip 1.0, is
refused: the LAZ specification calls it outdated and leaves it out, and the LASzip paper says it does not describe it.
Reading LASzip's source to get it is what this file rules out.
The LAZ specification PDF was read from downloads.rapidlasso.de (public format description, no code).

## Cutscenes: `@margoengine/cutscene`

Written clean room from the public descriptions below. No source code of sequencers or engines (Unity Timeline, Unreal
Sequencer, Godot AnimationPlayer, Theatre.js, GSAP, three.js, Babylon and the like) was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Camera paths | E. Catmull and R. Rom, "A class of local interpolating splines", Computer Aided Geometric Design, 1974 (uniform Catmull-Rom segments) |
| Rotation keys | K. Shoemake, "Animating rotation with quaternion curves", ACM SIGGRAPH 1985 (slerp), through `@margoengine/math`'s `interpolateQuaternions` |
| Roll | Rodrigues' rotation formula (standard linear algebra) |
| Easing | `@margoengine/tween`'s easings (Penner's equations, CSS Easing Functions Level 1 cubic-bezier) |
| Instants and ordering | Our own design: half-open clip spans, instants sorted once by time, kind and document order |
| Virtual camera shots (`cutscene/shots`) | Our own design from the public idea of cutting between named cameras on a timeline; the view blend is spherical linear interpolation of a direction (K. Shoemake, 1985) with its length blended apart; custom track types are ours. No source of Unity Cinemachine or Unreal Sequencer was read |
| Render to video (`cutscene/render`) | Fixed-step rendering through `@margoengine/headless/video`; the sound track follows RFC 7845 (Ogg Opus, for `OpusHead`), RFC 9559 and the WebM guidelines, Encapsulation of Opus in ISO Base Media (opus-codec.org) and ISO/IEC 14496-1, 14496-3 and 14496-14 for `esds`, and ISO/IEC 14496-12 for the edit list (`elst`) that cuts an AAC encoder's lead-in, measured by putting seeded noise through the runtime's own encoder and decoder; the mix is plain sums and straight-line resampling |
| Take recorder (`cutscene/record`) | Key thinning by D. Douglas and T. Peucker, "Algorithms for the reduction of the number of points", The Canadian Cartographer, 1973 (after U. Ramer, 1972), measured on the track's own interpolation; input through `@margoengine/input/replay` |
| Lip sync (`cutscene/lipsync`) | Linear prediction by J. Makhoul, "Linear prediction: a tutorial review", Proceedings of the IEEE, 1975; the vowel chart of G. Peterson and H. Barney, "Control methods used in a study of the vowels", JASA, 1952; ARPAbet symbols as the CMU Pronouncing Dictionary lists them; VRM 1.0 preset names and Apple's published `ARFaceAnchor.BlendShapeLocation` names; test speech from CMU ARCTIC (notice beside the files) |
| VFX and activation tracks (`cutscene/vfx`) | Replay of a deterministic simulation from its last empty state: Margo's own design over `@margoengine/vfx`, with no outside source |
| Timeline editor (`cutscene/editor`) | Plain DOM, WAI-ARIA 1.2 roles (`slider`, `toolbar`, `group`), Pointer Events and `EditHistory` of `@margoengine/inspector`; the layout is the common one of rows under a ruler, from no source |

## Cinematic cameras: `@margoengine/cinematic`

Written clean room from the published work below. No source of a camera system in any engine or add-on was read.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Camera brain (`cinematic/brain`) | Priorities and a selector over many cameras: our own design from the public idea, as surveyed by M. Christie, P. Olivier and J.-M. Normand, "Camera Control in Computer Graphics", Computer Graphics Forum 27(8), 2008. View blend: spherical linear interpolation of a direction (K. Shoemake, 1985), with the length mixed. The blend stack and the screen up carried through a pole are our own design |
| Framing composer (`cinematic/composer`) | Solving the aim from where a subject should appear on screen: J. Blinn, "Where am I? What am I looking at?", IEEE Computer Graphics and Applications 8(4), 1988. The closed form for pitch and heading with no roll, the zones and the frame-rate independent decay are our own derivation; the dead and soft zones are the public idea, with no source read |
| Dolly tracks (`cinematic/dolly`) | Nearest point by `closestPointOnSpline` of `@margoengine/splines`. Golden-section search for the window: J. Kiefer, "Sequential minimax search for a maximum", Proceedings of the American Mathematical Society, 1953. Our own design otherwise |
| Group framing (`cinematic/group`) | The distance and field of view that fit a sphere come from the distance of a point to the planes of the view frustum, our own derivation from standard geometry. Framing several subjects from one view is the public problem surveyed by Christie, Olivier and Normand, 2008 |
| Noise profiles (`cinematic/noise`) | One dimensional gradient noise: K. Perlin, "An image synthesizer", SIGGRAPH 1985, with the quintic fade of K. Perlin, "Improving noise", ACM Transactions on Graphics 21(3), 2002; the hash and the two lattice points a cycle are our own. Rotation: Rodrigues' formula. Preset frequencies: hand tremor of 5 to 12 Hz as reported in "The hand tremor spectrum is modified by the inertial sensor mass", Scientific Reports, 2022; body heave near 1.75 Hz and wheel hop of 10 to 15 Hz as measured in "Vertical accelerations measured in several types of car travelling over surface discontinuities", Transport and Road Research Laboratory, 1975; a step near 2.0 Hz from A. Pachi and T. Ji, "Frequency and velocity of people walking", The Structural Engineer 83(3), 2005. Amplitudes are by eye |
| Confiner volumes (`cinematic/confine`) | The even-odd rule for a point in a polygon, as set out in M. Shimrat, "Algorithm 112: Position of point relative to polygon", Communications of the ACM, 1962. Nearest point on a segment and the meeting of two offset lines: standard geometry. The push from the walls is our own |
| Focus tracking (`cinematic/lens`) | Plane of focus along the view axis, and field of view from subject size: the thin lens and pinhole relations, as `@margoengine/cameras/physical` already uses them. The rack and the zoom are exponential decays of our own |

## Navigation: `@margoengine/navmesh`

Written clean room from the published work below. No source of a navmesh library or another engine was read; Recast and Detour were not opened. The batch 167 timing drives recast-navigation 0.44.0 as a black box. The config field names are the ones in its README and generators API (https://github.com/isaac-mason/recast-navigation-js). The library source was not opened. The earlier parts of this package (ORCA, the funnel, the voxel pipeline) cite their sources in `packages/navmesh/src/`.

| Area | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Tiles (`navmesh/tiles`) | A Margo design: a tile re-runs the pipeline of `./build` on its own cells and a border, and the border, the kept cells and the stitching of polygon edges on a tile line are ours. Related work, read as far as its section 6 and not built from: W. G. van Toll, A. F. Cook IV, R. Geraerts, "A navigation mesh for dynamic environments", Computer Animation and Virtual Worlds 23(5-6), 2012, which repairs a medial axis mesh. |
| Search from portals (`navmesh`, all shapes) | Search states at the midpoints of polygon edges: M. Kallmann, "Path planning in triangulations", IJCAI workshop on reasoning, representation and learning in computer games, 2005, known here only through its description in D. Demyen and M. Buro, "Efficient Triangulation-Based Pathfinding", AAAI 2006 (read in full). Kallmann's paper could not be had: its host did not answer and the web archive was offline on 2026-11-07. Demyen and Buro search triangles with bounds on the entry edge and rerun the funnel until the bound proves the best route; Margo keeps one pass, so a route is not proved shortest. Costing a polygon as the walk from entry to exit and aiming the estimate at the end point are a Margo design on top of that. |
| Agent types and filters (`navmesh/agents`) | One mesh per body size, and the cost of keeping one, as D. Demyen and M. Buro state it in "Efficient Triangulation-Based Pathfinding", AAAI 2006 (read in full): growing the obstacles by the body works, but must be redone per size. Their width per triangle does not fit a voxel mesh, so Margo keeps a mesh per size. Costs per area multiply the polygon cost, and a multiplier of 1 or more keeps the straight line estimate a lower bound, which is what P. E. Hart, N. J. Nilsson, B. Raphael, "A formal basis for the heuristic determination of minimum cost paths", IEEE Trans. SSC-4(2), 1968, Theorem 1 asks (read in full); filters below 1 are refused for that reason. The area boxes, filters and types are a Margo design. |
| Baked files (`navmesh/codec`) | The file layout is a Margo design and is written out in the package README. The checksum is the CRC-32 of ISO 3309 (polynomial 0xEDB88320 in its reflected form), the one PNG and zip use. |
| Jump links (`navmesh/links`) | M. van de Kerkhof, "Automated generation of jump links for navigation meshes", MSc thesis, Utrecht University, 2016: the jump parameters (Definition 4.2), the arc (Definition 4.3, Equation 1), the land position (Definition 4.4), valid jumps (Definition 4.5), the search range and the step size. Read in sections 1 to 9 and the references. Its links are point to segment on a voxel field and its prototype does not dilate solids; Margo samples mesh edges, tests the whole cylinder exactly against the triangles and keeps point to point links, so its section 6 is not built from. Work the thesis cites by Mononen and by Sunshine-Hill is known here only through it. |
| Hierarchy (`navmesh/hierarchy`) | A. Botea, M. Mueller, J. Schaeffer, "Near optimal hierarchical path-finding", Journal of Game Development 1(1), 2004 (read in full): clusters, entrances, transition points, cached intra-cluster edges, inserting the two ends per query, abstract search, refinement.
